xviii    7831 0281–034
Section 1
Operator Environment

         This section gives you an overview of the operator environment by introducing the
         hardware, concepts, and tools you need to operate the system effectively. It
         describes the responsibilities that are typically handled by the system operator,
         provides an overview of the system hardware, and introduces the system software.
         Detailed information about the hardware and software is available in other manuals.
         Refer to the manuals listed in Appendix B.


1.1. About This Manual
         This manual describes operator responsibilities and procedures for OS 2200 systems.
         It provides basic concepts and objectives of system operations and gives specific
         operating procedures.

Documentation Updates
         This document contains all the information that was available at the time of
         publication. Changes identified after release of this document are included in problem
         list entry (PLE) 19251489. To obtain a copy of the PLE, contact your Unisys service
         representative or access the current PLE from the Unisys Product Support website:

            http://www.support.unisys.com/all/ple/19251489

         Note: If you are not logged into the Product Support site, you will be asked to do
         so.

Audience
         System operators are the primary audience for this manual. System administrators
         and programmers might also need to read and refer to this manual.

         Anyone using this manual must be familiar with mainframe computer operation and
         terminology, and have a basic understanding of Executive Control Language (ECL) and
         File Utility Routines/Program File Utility Routines (FURPUR).




7831 0281–034                                                                                1–1
Operator Environment




How to Use This Manual
        This manual is designed to be used primarily as a reference. Sections 1 through 9
        include the basic concepts and objectives of system operation and specific operating
        procedures. Section 10 lists the unsolicited keyins according to function, and Section
        11 provides detailed information about each keyin.

        The System Console Messages Reference Manual (7833 3275) must be used in
        conjunction with this manual. It lists and explains console messages, provides system
        error codes, and describes other types of errors.

Notation Conventions
        This manual uses these conventions for keyins and messages:

           Uppercase letters
            Indicates the exact name of the operator command, also known as a keyin, for
            example: UP
           Uppercase letters with underscores
            Indicates a system generation parameter, for example: SENTRY_CONTROL
           Lowercase italicized letters
            Indicates a variable name, for example: cmod
           Leading zero on addresses
            Indicates an octal address, for example: 077
           Small braces and bar
            Indicates that the parameter can have one of two values, for example: {UP|DN}
           Brackets
            Indicates optional parameter, for example: [,options]
           Brackets and bar
            Indicates optional parameter; you can select one of the indicated values for
            example [STD|SHARED]
           Subscript numbers on variable names
            Denotes that there can be several values for the same parameter, for example:
            devnam1
           Ellipsis (...)
            Indicates that more values for the same parameter can be added, for example:
            devnam1,devnam2,...,devnamn




1–2                                                                             7831 0281–034
                                                                              Operator Environment




Variable Names
         Following is a list of the most common variable names used throughout this manual.


                   Symbolic Name                                    Meaning

             Cmod                       channel module
             Cu                         control unit
             Devnam                     device name
             dir-id                     directory identifier (for example, SHARED# or STD#)
             F-cycle                    file cycle
             host-id                    host identifier (for example, A, B, C, and so forth)
             iop                        input/output processor
             ip                         instruction processor
             mmod                       memory module
             pack-id                    pack identifier
             run-id                     run identifier
             site-id                    site identifier
             sname                      symbiont name
             user-id                    user identifier


         This is an example of a keyin using a variable name:

             DN ip


1.2. Operator Responsibilities
         The operator responsibilities described here relate to a Unisys OS 2200 system.
         Although this manual contains specific information to adequately perform some of the
         responsibilities, you have to refer to other Unisys publications for detailed information.

         The operator responsibilities for a Unisys system include the following:

             Initialize the system software and install and initialize products and application
              software
              System start-up and initialization is the process that brings a system to the ready
              state. It includes powering up system components and loading software. Section
              3 provides general information about system start-up and references manuals that
              contain specific information for some systems. See Section 8 for system security
              file initialization procedures.




7831 0281–034                                                                                      1–3
Operator Environment




           Respond to system messages
            System messages are sent to the system console to inform the operator of
            system activity or to request that the operator perform some action, such as
            loading a specific tape. Section 2 includes information about the system console,
            system messages, and other aspects of console operation.
           Enter jobs into the system and check their status
            This activity is done as part of monitoring system operations and in response to
            requests from system users. Section 4 includes an overview of the keyins used
            for controlling job input and checking their status.
           Monitor system status and throughput
            General system status information is continuously displayed on the system
            console. Keyins let the operator display more detailed information, make entries in
            the system log concerning system activity, and adjust the job mix for efficient
            operation. The status of system components, such as printers (and their print
            queues) and disks (and storage allocation), is displayed in response to keyins.
            Section 4 includes information about the keyins used for status and throughput
            operations.
           Mount and dismount tapes
            Tapes are mounted and dismounted in response to system messages. Section 5
            describes how to respond to tape messages and includes information on Tape
            Automatic Volume Recognition (TAVR), a feature that lets the operator mount
            (premount) tapes before they are requested.
           Prep disks
            Disks can be either fixed or removable. They can be reserved for specific
            assignments, and removable disks can be removed from one system and put on
            another. Information about disk packs and keyins used with disks is included in
            Section 5.
           Collect and sort printed output
            If your system is configured to label user printouts, see Section 8.

           Back up system software
            Backing up system software on a regular basis minimizes damage that can be
            done by system crashes, failed recoveries, and other system problems. It lets you
            recover more quickly and with greater confidence. See the File Administration
            System (FAS) Operations Guide (7830 7972) for more information on doing
            system software backups.
           Dump memory
            System dumps can be performed to troubleshoot system errors. They can be
            started either manually or automatically. Section 6 describes the procedures for
            performing and processing system dumps.




1–4                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                                                         Operator Environment




            Recover the system if it fails
             Recovery of the system generally refers to software recovery after a system
             failure. Integrated Recovery and the Integrated Recovery utility, as well as audit
             trails, are some of the features that streamline system recovery. Section 3
             describes general recovery procedures and Section 7 provides information on
             audit trails and Step Control options, which are used in Integrated Recovery.

         In addition to the tasks just listed, you need to be familiar with the following:

            Objectives, procedures, and standards at your site
            Efficient operation of hardware components
            Input, output, and processing requirements of the operating system
            Recognizing inefficient system operation and determining corrective action
            System operations in a multihost operations environment and how it differs from
             a one-host environment, if your site has a multihost environment.

1.3. System Hardware Components
         Operating a Unisys computer system requires you to interact with the system
         hardware in a variety of ways: system start-up, system shutdown, loading tapes,
         preparing disks, and so on. This subsection provides a general overview of system
         hardware, identifying the components and their function. Specific information about
         powering on the components, identifying the location of switches and indicators, and
         so on is covered in the hardware manuals shipped with the component.

1.3.1.   Central Electronics Complex (CEC)
         The two major components of a central electronic complex (CEC) are the processing
         complex and the I/O complex, described below. The system can optionally contain an
         Extended Transaction Processor (XPC). Refer to the XPC-L System Operations Guide
         (3850 7422) for more information.

Processing Complex
         The processing complex contains Processing Complex Cabinets (PCC), Processor
         Cooling Units (PCU), and Modular Power Centers (MPC) or Power Peripheral
         Distribution units (PPDU).

         Each processing complex cabinet contains

            Instruction processors: The instruction processor executes program and system
             software instructions.
            Storage controller: The storage controller interfaces the instruction processors
             and the I/O complex with main storage. Storage controllers allow multiple PCCs
             and I/O complex cabinets to be configured as balanced multiprocessor systems.




7831 0281–034                                                                                 1–5
Operator Environment




           Main Storage Units (MSU): Each storage controller has a direct interface with two
            independent MSUs.
           Network Interface Module (NIM): The NIM connects the PCC with the system
            control facility for system operations.

        The processing complex also contains the processor cooling unit and the modular
        power center. The PCU provides liquid cooling for the PCC, circulating water cooled by
        an external source. One PCU is required for each PCC.

        The MPC or PPDU provides an isolation transformer, circuit breakers, and
        voltage-matching facilities for the central electronics complex.

I/O Complex
        The I/O complex includes I/O complex cabinets (ICC) and disk subsystem adapter
        cabinets (DSAC). These units provide the system interface and the I/O channels to
        support peripheral equipment. The channels provide the final hardware link to the disk,
        tape, printer, and communications peripherals. They also manage and control transfers
        to and from the peripherals.

        Each I/O complex cabinet contains

           I/O remote adapter: The I/O remote adapter provides the main storage interface to
            the ICC.
           I/O processor modules: I/O processor modules control the initiation, transfer, and
            termination of data sequences to specific channels in the ICC.
           Block multiplexer channel interfaces: BMCs provide the final hardware link to the
            disk, tape, printers, and communications peripherals. They manage and control
            transfers to and from the peripherals.
           An interface for each ICC to support one optional DSAC.
        Each DSAC contains word channel modules used for the connection and control of
        peripheral devices requiring word channel interfaces.

        See your System Capabilities Overview for more information.

1.3.2. Extended Processing Complex (XPC-L)
        Extended Processing Complex (XPC-L) is an optional hardware device that connects to
        all hosts in an Extended Transaction Capacity (XTC) system. In a one-host system, file
        and record locking is handled through software locking by TIP File Control for TIP files
        or by UDS Control for UDS/TIP files. In an XTC system, XPC-L provides hardware
        locking outside the hosts to allow access to shared databases regardless of the
        database type. In addition, the XPC-L provides interhost communications.




1–6                                                                              7831 0281–034
                                                                      Operator Environment




1.3.3. Server Sentinel
         The Server Sentinel software provides system control processing. Server Sentinel
         software executes on the embedded Service Processors.

         Through the CSE, which provides access to hardware, the Server Sentinel software
         also communicates with the OS 2200 operating system and the CEC hardware.

1.3.4. Peripheral Subsystems
         The peripheral subsystems provide data storage and hard-copy output for user
         programs. Data storage is provided by tape subsystems and disk subsystems (mass
         storage). Printer subsystems provide hard-copy output.

Mass Storage (Disk Subsystems)
         Disk subsystems consist of the control units and their attached disk devices. The
         components of the disk subsystem are available in many configurations to meet a
         wide variety of needs.

         For specific information about the disk subsystem attached to your system, refer to
         the manuals provided with it.

Tape Subsystems
         Tape subsystems consist of the control units and their attached tape devices. Like
         disk subsystems, they are available in many configurations to meet a wide variety of
         needs. There are different types of tape devices and control units to handle reel and
         cartridge tapes of different densities.

         Section 5 contains information about the cartridge tape library (CTL), Tape Automatic
         Volume Recognition (TAVR), and the tape subsystem. Refer to documentation
         provided with the tape subsystem components for more information.

1.3.5. Communications Subsystems
         OS 2200 systems can include PCIOP, which is driven by the CPComm software
         product. Ethernet network interface cards can be attached to the PCIOP.


1.4. System Software
         System software includes the operating system of which the Exec is a part, and the
         various utilities, tools, and diagnostics that keep the system running so that user
         programs run properly. This manual documents the Exec and its features as they
         relate to system operation.

         The Exec controls the system operating environment. It processes runs, controls files,
         manages system resources, and performs input/output operations for users. Console
         software lets the operator interact with the Exec, through operator keyins, to monitor
         and control system operation.




7831 0281–034                                                                                1–7
Operator Environment




        The following subsections describe some of the Exec features you need to
        understand as an operator. Your system cannot have all these features because some
        of them are optional.

1.4.1. Operating System and the Exec
        The OS 2200 operating system supports batch, interactive, and transaction
        processing.

        The Exec provides the basic support for all central complex hardware and channel-
        connected peripherals. It also isolates user programs from direct interface with the
        hardware. At its lowest level, the Exec makes users aware of the hardware and lets
        them control it. Unisys device control software, such as Enterprise Output Manager,
        the printer control program, and the online diagnostic software generally use this level
        of interface. In higher level operations, the Exec takes complete control. Interfaces for
        tapes and disks, for example, do not require the user program to know the physical
        device characteristics at all.

        The Exec also controls how system resources are allocated. The primary resources
        are memory space, instruction processor time, mass storage space, tape drive and
        printer allocation, and network channels. Once resources are allocated, the Exec
        makes sure that programs do not interfere with one another or simultaneously modify
        the same data.

        Common security and limit controls are also handled by the Exec. Security prevents
        unauthorized access to programs or data. Limit controls (the Quota system) make
        certain that no single user exceeds the resource usage limits imposed by the system
        administrator.

        The operator keyins described in Section 11 let you control and modify the operational
        parameters of the operating system and request information from it.

1.4.2. Communications Software
        A suite of software products enables communications between programs executing
        on the ClearPath system and software executing in other computers, work stations,
        and devices. Communications Platform (CPComm) and SILAS provide the network
        protocol handling and control of the network connections.

        COMAPI provides a Sockets-like interface to CPComm and the network. CITA provides
        a TCP/IP path for TIP programs. The Message Control Bank (MCB) is the message
        handling and message recovery component of the OS 2200 Integrated Recovery
        system.

        Figure 1-1 shows the relationships among CPComm, SILAS, and the other
        communications software components.




1–8                                                                               7831 0281–034
                                                                                        Operator Environment




                                                 TIP


                   Other             MCB               COMPOOL                       Demand
                 Programs

                                   CITA
                File Transfer                               SILAS
                 Programs                                (INT1-DTPX
                                                                                              Other
                                 COMAPI                    or Telnet)                       Programs


                                            CPComm
                                          (TCP; UDP; Telnet;
                                             TP0-TCP)*                  *The caller of CPComm
                                                                        selects the protocol stack.
                                        Network Interface

                  Figure 1–1. Communications Products with CPComm

         Refer to the following documents for operational instructions for the communications
         software products:

            Communications Platform Configuration and Operations Guide (7844 8438)
            System Interface for Legacy Application Systems (SILAS) Configuration and
             Operations Guide (7851 5475)
            Communications Application Program Interface (COMAPI) User’s Guide
             (7851 5749)
            Communications Interface for Transaction Applications Configuration and
             Operations Guide (7862 6470)
            Message Control Bank (MCB) Configuration and Operations Guide (7833 1550)

1.4.3. Transaction Processing (TIP)
         Transaction Processing (referred to as TIP) is an extension of the Exec that provides a
         high-performance system in which a user causes the execution of a predefined
         transaction program by entering an input message from a remote terminal. The
         program typically accesses the application program database and returns a response
         to the user.

         The Exec supports the creation and maintenance of the database and program files for
         these applications programs. Step Control and Audit Control are important
         mechanisms for TIP that allow each program step to be completed and logged so that
         system failures do not jeopardize transactions.



7831 0281–034                                                                                            1–9
Operator Environment




        For more information about TIP, refer to the Transaction Processing Administration
        and Operations Reference Manual (7830 7881).

1.4.4. Multi-Host File Sharing (MHFS)
        Multi-host File Sharing (MHFS) is an Exec feature that allows file sharing between
        multiple OS 2200 systems. Each system in the MHFS environment is called a host.
        MHFS associates the files with a standard or shared master file directory and manages
        communications for file sharing within a host and across multiple hosts.

        Keyins are available that enable you to change the MHFS configuration by bringing
        hosts or shared devices up or down. See Section 9 for operations procedures within a
        multihost environment. See the Multi-Host File Sharing (MHFS) Planning, Installation,
        and Operations Guide (7830 7469) for more information on MHFS.

1.4.5. Extended Transaction Capacity (XTC)
        Extended Transaction Capacity (XTC) is an optional group of features that expands
        transaction processing capacity into multiple host systems. XTC provides for shared,
        synchronized, logical record access for multiple hosts between transactions and
        databases on shared mass storage. The application groups that simultaneously access
        the shared mass storage databases are called concurrent application groups. The
        XPC-L is a required central complex hardware device connected to all hosts in an XTC
        configuration that synchronizes locking for concurrent access to shared mass storage
        databases.

        See Section 9 for operations procedures within a multihost environment. See the
        Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for Multihost
        Environments (7831 0919) for more information on XTC.

1.4.6. Integrated Recovery
        Integrated Recovery synchronizes database and message recovery after a failure.
        Recovery features work in combination to expedite the recovery process, minimize
        recovery difficulties, regain system stability, and ensure data integrity. The major Exec
        components of Integrated Recovery are Exec Step Control, Exec Audit Control, and
        TIP File Control.

        Integrated Recovery encompasses the features necessary to provide the database
        recovery needs of the Exec, TIP, and UDS, as well as the message recovery needs of
        MCB.

        Integrated Recovery is available only for user sessions or programs associated with an
        application group. When you reboot the system after a failure or after bringing the
        system down, the Exec initiates most of the necessary recovery actions for each
        application group. The Exec either determines the appropriate action or prompts you
        to define the appropriate action. When necessary, the Exec also prompts you to start
        the Integrated Recovery Utility (formerly IRU) short recovery to bring up an application
        group.




1–10                                                                              7831 0281–034
                                                                         Operator Environment




         When the recovery action initiated by the Exec cannot recover the system to its
         previous state, Integrated Recovery Utility provides other methods to recover the
         system, such as medium or long recovery.

         Refer to the Integrated Recovery Conceptual Overview (7830 8186) for information
         about Integrated Recovery concepts and procedures.

Step Control
         Step Control is an Exec Integrated Recovery component that synchronizes database
         and message recovery activities for user programs associated with a particular
         application group. It also provides central control information for the application
         group’s Integrated Recovery software components so appropriate recovery actions
         can take place.

         A step is a recoverable or nonrecoverable unit of work. It represents a portion of
         processing associated with a user program. A recoverable step is a unit of
         recoverable processing (for example, a sequence of user program commands that
         update a database). If a failure occurs before a recoverable step point is reached, the
         database updates associated with the step are discarded and the program rolls back
         (reverts) to the point just after the program’s last recoverable step.

         Section 7 provides information on the Step Control initialization and recovery options.

Audit Control and Audit Trail
         Audit Control is an Exec component that initializes, creates, maintains, and controls
         access to an application group’s audit trail. In addition, it manages audit trail recoveries
         and audit trail keyins. See Section 7 for Audit Control operations procedures.

         An audit trail is a continuous and chronological sequence of audit records stored in
         mass storage or a tape file. In general, the audit record represents a recoverable
         event. Audit trails are associated with complexes within the Exec and manage all
         aspects of data storage on behalf of their associated complexes.

         The complexes that use audit trails are:

            System log
            Integrated Recovery Step Control
            Integrated Recovery Transaction Performance Monitor (TPM)
            Capacity on Demand (COD)

         Audit trails can be configured as simplex or duplex (one data file or two data files).
         Individual audit trails can also be configured to use tape or mass storage.




7831 0281–034                                                                                     1–11
Operator Environment




Integrated Recovery Utility
        The Integrated Recovery Utility (IRU) plays a key role in an Integrated Recovery
        system. System software components such as the Exec, TIP File Control, UDS, and
        MCB have built-in procedures to handle most failures. If these recovery features fail,
        however, you can use Integrated Recovery Utility to perform alternate recovery
        procedures.

        IRU is a software tool that combines and uses information available from other
        Integrated Recovery components to recover files and databases to ensure their
        integrity. Often, the work IRU does depends on the success or failure of the other
        Integrated Recovery system components. IRU might be considered a
        contingency-action product in recovery situations. You use it when automatic
        safeguard or recovery actions of the other components fail.

        IRU supports two environments: single-host and Extended Transaction Capacity (XTC).
        IRU also offers three methods of recovery: short, medium, and long. Refer to the
        Integrated Recovery Utility Operations Guide (7830 8194) and the Integrated
        Recovery Conceptual Overview (7830 8186) for complete information.

1.4.7. Security
        The security officer is responsible for security administration and planning. If you have
        specific questions about security operations, ask your site’s security officer.

        OS 2200 security is organized into following levels, with site configuration options
        within each level:

           Fundamental Security
            Fundamental Security, a standard feature of OS 2200, offers basic protection for
            the ClearPath enterprise computing environment. Fundamental Security
            establishes security controls at the system level and provides limited additional
            security based on an individual user’s identity.
           Security Level 1 provides additional protections beyond Fundamental Security. It
            includes file ownership, clearance levels, access control records (ACRs),
            assignment of extra processing rights to individual users, TIP file security,
            enhanced auditing capabilities, and other security features.
           Additional security levels (2 and 3) can provide an even more rigidly protected
            environment for sites with more stringent security requirements.
        For a comprehensive overview of OS 2200 security, see the ClearPath Enterprise
        Servers OS 2200 Security Overview (8205 7282).

        As a system operator, you need to know about the level that is installed at your site
        and you need to understand the impact it has on your responsibilities, primarily in
        booting the system and labeling tapes and mass storage files. You must also be
        certain you have the proper security privileges to perform operations with Integrated
        Recovery, use the TS keyin, and boot the system.




1–12                                                                              7831 0281–034
                                                                       Operator Environment




         The security administrator can use any of the following products. In this document,
         the term "security management product" refers to one of the following products:

            Apex Administration and Reporting is composed of two major components. An
             agent, APEXAG, is installed on the OS 2200 server, and a Web server service is
             installed on a Windows server. The user interface uses Web browser technology,
             thereby making any device that can access the Web server a potential system
             management workstation. A command line utility, @SECMGR, released with Apex,
             provides a batch/demand interface to perform those same administrator or end-
             user operations. For more information, see the ClearPath OS 2200 Apex Getting
             Started Guide (8207 4055), or ClearPath OS 2200 Apex Help (8207 4154).
            TeamQuest® Site Management Complex (SIMAN) provides a menu-driven
             interface for creating and maintaining security and resource control in an OS 2200
             system. SIMAN can be run interactively in an OS 2200 demand session. SIMAN
             includes a batch processor whose command syntax is compatible with @SECMGR.
             For more information, see the TeamQuest Site Management Complex (SIMAN)
             Administration and End Use Reference Manual (TQ-01151).

1.4.8. Unit Duplexing
         Unit Duplexing is an Exec feature that reduces system vulnerability to mass storage
         failures. It consists of a hardware configuration in which each mass storage device has
         an associated backup device that is transparent to all end-user software and to most
         system software.

         If a user program issues a request to read or write to a file on a unit-duplexed device,
         the write request is automatically performed on both devices and the read request is
         performed on either device. From a software point of view, the unit-duplexed devices
         are bit-for-bit identical.

         For additional information, refer to the Unit Duplexing Planning, Installation, and
         Operations Overview (7830 7592).


1.5. Operator Profile
         System administrators have the option of setting up an operator profile for the system
         operators through Apex or TeamQuest SIMAN. This operator profile consists of an
         array of strings, called environmental variables, which contain information that defines
         operators, applications, and the system. Included in this profile is an environmental
         variable that lets operators receive messages from the Extended Language Message
         System (ELMS) in their native language, or another language, as determined by the
         site. Exec messages are currently displayed only in English.

         The profile is set up at system initialization time using the security management
         product. After system initialization, profile changes are made through an Exec call.
         Refer to the ClearPath OS 2200 Apex Help (8207 4154), TeamQuest Site Management
         Complex (SIMAN) Administration and End Use Reference Manual (TQ-01151) or the
         System Services Programming Reference Manual (7833 4455) for more information.

Operator Environment




1–14                   7831 0281–034
Section 2
System Console

         The system console is used to control and monitor system performance. Its
         configuration is governed by rules that are unique to each system.

         On OS 2200 systems, workstations running Server Sentinel and Operations Sentinel
         provide the operations functions.


2.1. Console Operations Software
         Unisys supports Server Sentinel and Operations Sentinel.

2.1.1.   Server Sentinel
         You perform many operations tasks on OS 2200 systems through the Server Sentinel
         interface. The Server Sentinel interface can run on the master Service Processor or on
         remote workstations.

         The general functions you perform are as follows:

            Start and stop the partitions and their operating systems.
            Obtain information about the state of the server, partitions, and hardware units.
            Reconfigure partitions and their hardware units to add new units, remove failed
             units, restore units, or reconfigure hardware resources by assigning them to
             different partitions.

2.1.2. Operations Sentinel
         Operations Sentinel enables an operator to monitor and control the following systems
         and servers:

            Dorado Series servers
             These servers consist of OS 2200, UNIX, Linux, and Windows partitions.
            ClearPath NX, ClearPath LX, and ClearPath Enterprise Server Libra Series servers,
             and FS and EA series of MCP servers
             These servers consist of MCP, UNIX, Linux, and Windows partitions or systems.




7831 0281–034                                                                                    2–1
System Console




          ES7000 servers
           These servers consist of Windows, UNIX, and Linux partitions.
          Windows systems
           These include systems running Windows Server 2008 (Standard Edition), Windows
           7 Professional, Windows 2008/R2, or Windows 8 Professional.
          UNIX systems
          Linux systems
          Other systems compliant with ANSI X3.64 protocol
       Operations Sentinel also provides interfaces to Server Sentinel, NetIQ AppManager,
       and HP OpenView Network Node Manager including the Windows, Sun Solaris, and
       HP-UX versions. See the Operations Sentinel Software Release Announcement (7862
       6512) for more information.

Operations Sentinel Console
       Operations Sentinel Console is the principal client application of Operations Sentinel. It
       is the main user interface for operators to manage the various systems from a single
       workstation. Operations Sentinel Console provides the following capabilities:

          Topological displays of the systems you are managing. The status of each system,
           partition, and connection is indicated graphically.
          The means to open console windows, terminal windows, and remote desktops for
           the managed systems and partitions with simple mouse clicks.
          The means to launch site-defined external applications for the managed systems
           and partitions with simple mouse clicks.
          The console allows you to generate a system report of multiple or single OS 2200,
           MCP, Windows, and UNIX/Linux Systems. The report consists of bar charts where
           each bar chart is plotted for each monitored system for each system resource
           against a refresh interval.
          Alert icons that summarize the alert states of the managed systems and partitions.
           The appearance of these alert icons indicates the presence and severity of
           outstanding alerts.
          An Alerts window that lists all outstanding alerts. You select which alert attributes
           appear in this window and then you can clear and acknowledge the alerts and
           display detailed information about them.
          Tables that depict utilization properties for managed systems in real-time.
          The means to specify thresholds for system utilization properties, so that an alert
           is raised automatically when the value of a property goes out of acceptable range.
          The means to start other client applications of Operations Sentinel.

2.2. Keyin Format and Masking Rules
         Masking characters are relational expressions with string attributes (the percent (%)
         character) that mask portions of the value of the expression.

         ID fields in the following keyins can contain masking or wildcard characters, or both.
         The asterisk (*) is the defined wildcard character and matches any sequence of
         characters. The question mark (?) is the defined mask character and matches any
         character or a blank character. The ID fields also support embedded masking.

         Examples:

             T A*                         Lists all runs starting with A.
             T *A?                        Lists all runs that end with A or have an A as the
                                          penultimate character.
             T,B D?                       Batch run D. Lists all runs starting with D and
                                          whose run ID is one or two characters long.
             T,U C?X                      Lists all runs executing under a user ID that
                                          starts with C, ends with X, and is three
                                          characters long.


         If no arguments are specified, a full mask is implied.

         Example:

         "T" is same as "T *" which is also same as "T ??????".

         If multiple fields are specified, an implied OR is assumed. If negation is specified
         through the N option, an implied AND is assumed.

             T D SEC,PROD                 Implied OR, that is run SEC, run PROD, or both, if
                                          both exist.
             T,N D SEC,PROD               Implied AND, that is NOT run SEC AND NOT run
                                          PROD.


         Note: The use of wildcard characters, masking characters, the user ID (U) and
         negation (N) options are controlled by Exec configuration parameters. If the Exec is
         not configured to enable some or all of these features, an error message is
         displayed.

2.3. Entering Unsolicited Keyins
       Unsolicited keyins are commands that submit information to or make inquiries of the
       system. They are called unsolicited keyins because the system does not prompt you
       to enter information or respond to a message.

       Unsolicited keyins are used to inform the system of changes in the external
       environment, request information about the internal system activity, or modify the
       internal system activity. Section 11 contains a list and description of all the unsolicited
       keyins. Use these keyins carefully—some slow down system performance.

       Keyins are composed of the 1- to 8-character command (for example, AC or IOPHFL),
       and for some keyins, a single space, a comma and space, or a comma (depending on
       the keyin), and one or more parameters.

       These keyins illustrate the various types of formats:
            MS
            CH H
            FS,TAPES
            FS ALL,component
            MH FS,HOST
            LG text

       The maximum length of a keyin (including all parameters) is 76 characters.

       To enter a keyin from a local console, press the MSG (message) WAIT key on the
       keyboard. This stops output to the console subsystem and allows input. The contents
       of the screen scroll upward, and you see the start-of-entry character in the bottom left
       corner of the console. When you have completed the keyin, press the transmit key.
       The console routine sets a 1-minute cycle during which you can make entries.

       You can also enter a keyin by moving the cursor to any previous input line (a line that
       already has a start-of-entry character). When you enter new data, place the cursor
       immediately following the last existing character. When your entry is complete, press
       the transmit key.




2–6                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                                                            System Console




2.4. Console Messages
         Console messages generated by the Exec fall into one of four groups:

            System messages – General, informational, and system request
            Input/output messages – About peripheral device operations
            Communications messages – Concerning the communications complex
            Hardware confidence messages – About hardware status

         There are also four additional message groups available for your site that are
         undefined and can be used by your site for specific system operations. Your site can
         define these additional console message groups during system generation.

         Refer to the System Console Messages Reference Manual (7833 3275) for detailed
         information about message types and how to respond to them.


2.5. Controlling the System Console
         The following keyins let you control the system console:

            The AC keyin sets or clears the message groups that is displayed on different
             console devices, and lists the consoles that are configured to display messages.
            The CMR keyin controls the console message redisplay feature that lets you
             redisplay messages that have scrolled off the screen.
            The MR keyin, from remote displays, recalls outstanding messages that have
             scrolled off the screen. Local displays keep the read-and-reply messages from
             scrolling off the screen.
            The TS keyin checks which terminals are disabled and restores terminals.
         Refer to the keyin modules in Section 11 for a complete description of each keyin and
         its operation.


2.6. Remote Consoles
         Keyins can be entered at remote consoles under control of the security management
         product. Demand terminal users can receive console output, answer console
         messages, and enter certain unsolicited console keyins using the @@CONS statement.
         Using the security management product, the site administrator gives each user one of
         five remote console mode capability levels, and gives the user the ability to perform
         either the maximum or a subset of the maximum of the keyins allowed in a particular
         level.
         See the Executive Control Language (ECL) and FURPUR Reference Manual (7830
         7949) for a description of the five console mode capability levels and a list of the
         keyins that can be performed by demand users, along with the console capability level
         required for each keyin.




7831 0281–034                                                                                2–7
System Console




       When a remote console is in use, the status of the system can change without
       warning. You can have to respond to changes in unit status and print files that are
       deleted, as well as runs that terminate in error or are removed. You can also be
       required to respond to read-and-reply messages that have been routed to you from a
       terminated remote console.
       If the system configuration parameter is set to echo remote console messages on the
       system console (echo_remote_console), the local system console receives messages
       sent to the remote console. If the message is a read-and-reply, then it only displays as
       read-and-reply on the remote console. On the local system console, it is displayed as a
       read-only message.


2.7. Console Faults
       When a fault occurs on a console or console printer, an interlock message appears on
       all other available console devices. The message that is displayed depends on the type
       of fault. (Refer to the System Console Messages Reference Manual (7833 3275) to
       interpret these messages.)

       If necessary, you can redirect messages to other console devices with the AC keyin.


                                             Caution
            Do not use the XB control page parameter, which changes the border color,
            when operating the console. It destroys the current screen.



       After the fault, the system starts a 30-second cycle to retry output. Distribution of
       output to other devices on the console continues. When the condition is corrected,
       you can see backed-up messages on the corrected device, and normal output
       resumes.

       Read-and-reply messages might appear with different message numbers, and only the
       last line of multiline read-and-reply messages is displayed. Also, read-only messages
       that were on display before the fault are lost. Use the MR or CMR keyin to redisplay
       these messages.




2–8                                                                             7831 0281–034
Section 3
System Start-Up and Recovery
Procedures

         This section describes system start-up and recovery procedures (called initial boot and
         recovery boot, respectively). Figure 3-1 illustrates the types of system boots you can
         perform, whether they are performed from tape or mass storage and whether they
         are performed manually or automatically.


                                        System Boot




                              Initial Boot     Recovery Boot




                                                           From
                                         From Tape
                                                        Mass Storage




                                                 Performed        Performed
                                                 Manually        Automatically


                           Figure 3–1. Types of System Boots


3.1. Loading System Software
         The initial loading of software on the system, termed the initial boot, loads the Exec
         software into memory and starts the system running.

         For an initial boot, you must load the Exec from a tape. If errors occur during this
         process, you can dump the contents of main memory. The dump is then used to help
         identify what caused the errors. See Section 6 for information on how to perform and
         process system dumps.




7831 0281–034                                                                                 3–1
System Start-Up and Recovery Procedures




         The Exec does not perform the entire initial boot procedure automatically. Several
         pauses occur, and you are given a chance to make various changes. During these
         pauses, the system displays several informational messages and several
         read-and-reply messages. For more information about system generation, see the
         Exec System Software Installation and Configuration Guide (7830 7915).

         During the boot process, it might be necessary for you to identify the hardware that is
         available for use by the system if either of these situations occurs:

            The system that you want to load the Exec into does not contain all the
             components specified when your site generated the system (the configuration is
             different).
            You want to prohibit or restrict access to one or more components from the
             configuration to complete the load process.

         Once the Exec has been loaded, it takes control of the system. If the Exec hangs,
         crashes, or must be brought down for some other reason, it is typically restarted from
         mass storage with a recovery boot to restart the system.

         The boot and initialization elements of the Exec contain routines that do the following:

            Perform the initial load from tape
            Write the Exec on the system disk
            Load and execute the Exec from the system disk
            Take a system dump (in the event of failure)
            Perform an automatic recovery boot from mass storage ("automatic disk
             recovery") when the system dump is complete

         Both the initial boot and the recovery procedures are described in this section. See
         Section 9 for the system security file initialization procedures.

         Note: Cross-booting to a system with fewer mass storage devices is allowed. See
         PLE 17809598 for more information on this functionality.

3.1.1.   Jump Keys and System Boot
         Once the Exec system software is loaded, you can use a manual boot from disk or
         tape if required, but typically you can use an automatic boot when the system is
         already installed. This kind of boot performs an automatic disk recovery and quickly
         brings up the system.

         Jump keys control the action of the Exec during a boot (both an initial boot and a
         recovery boot). Use the SJ and CJ keyins to set or clear jump keys, and the DJ keyin
         to display which jump keys are set (see Section 11 for more information on these
         keyins). A jump key is not set or cleared until you receive a confirming message on
         the system console.




3–2                                                                               7831 0281–034
                                                         System Start-Up and Recovery Procedures




         There are 36 available jump keys in the system. Some are not used. Table 3–1 lists and
         explains the jump key settings:

                 Jump keys 1 to 16 are reserved for Unisys.
                 Jump keys 17 to 24 are available for users.
                 Jump keys 25 to 36 are reserved for Unisys debugging.

         Two jump keys, 9 and 13, can have undesirable consequences if you accidentally set
         them. For this reason, a message appears during the boot process asking you to
         confirm your action. Jump keys 9 and 13 are ignored during auto recovery.

         Before booting your system, the following jump key settings are recommended:

                 If this is an initial boot from tape, set jump keys 4 and 13.
                 If this is a tape recovery with library load, set jump key 4.
                 If you want a manual tape dump, set jump key 2. If you want a mass storage
                  dump, set jump key 2. (See Section 6 for more information on performing system
                  dumps.)


                                              Table 3–1. Jump Key Settings

                                                dump is taken. Should be cleared when the dump is complete.
             3           Auto Recovery          Disables autorecovery and disables automatic dump feature
                         Inhibit                after initialization.
             4           Library Reload         Loads processors and libraries from the boot tape during a
                                                tape boot. See also jump key 13.
             6           Full Dump              Performs a dump of all main storage, versus the selective
                                                dump that is the default dump type. The resulting dump is
                                                typically much larger when jump key 6 is set.
             7           TIP and Step           Solicits a read-and-reply message for TIP initialization. During a
                         Control                recovery boot, it also lets you choose between recovering and
                         Initialization and     initializing the pack status file.
                         Down Pack
                         Initialization
             9           Queue Recovery         Inhibits recovery of all run scheduling information, backlog runs
                         Inhibit                and print files. Ignored during autorecovery.
             10          Debug dump             If set, allows any application that executes an SGNL 10
                                                instruction to initiate an online dump. Useful when the Exec
                                                cannot detect an error but an application can.

System Start-Up and Recovery Procedures




                                      Table 3–1. Jump Key Settings


         Key No.   Jump Key Name                          Jump Key Task Description

                   only. Debug          executes an SGNL 10 instruction to trigger a system stop.
                   panic dump

                   breakpoint trace     the breakpoint trace mechanism to be used during the boot

                                                 System Start-Up and Recovery Procedures




3.1.2. Initial Boot
         The procedure for booting the Exec depends on the type of system and whether or
         not there are additional hardware components. The following information is intended
         only as a guide to booting the system.
         The instructions take you only to the point at which the Exec displays the first console
         message. Once you get to that point, the booting process is not yet complete;
         however, in most cases you must be able to weave your way through the console
         messages to complete it.
         When you load the Exec, the system displays messages that you must respond to. For
         example, the Exec can ask for the name of a device or request that you confirm your
         intent to perform some action. If you use products such as Transaction Processing
         (TIP) or the Integrated Recovery Utility (IRU), you can see messages that apply to
         these products.
         During a tape boot, the following steps occur for ClearPath IX systems:
         1.   The hardware loads the bootstrap code from the tape.
         2.   The bootstrap code loads the Exec from the tape into main storage.
         3.   The initialization portion of the Exec starts.
         4. The initialization portion initializes or recovers the mass storage file structure.
         5.   The Exec copies itself from the tape to the designated mass storage device.
         6. A runstream starts that loads the system libraries.

         The System Control Facility (SCF) is used for start-up and control of system partitions,
         which are defined in the Site Configuration Management System (SCMS II). To boot
         the system, you enter SCF commands from a system control display session; once the
         system is up, you can enter console keyins from a system operation display session
         to manage daily system activity, such as run scheduling and facility control.

         The procedure described here is the simplest one for performing an initial boot. Refer
         to the SCF Operations Guide for complete information on initial boot procedures and
         tape and disk recovery boot procedures and for a description of the SCF error
         messages that you might see during the system boot. Other boot messages are
         displayed by the Exec; see the System Console Messages Reference Manual (7833
         3275) for a description of those messages.

         1.   Press the F5 key to get to a system control display session.
         2.   From the system control menu, enter A to activate a partition and then select the
              partition. You are now in a Partition Control display session for that partition. The
              display shows the currently selected boot type, load path, and boot options for the
              partition.
         3.   Mount the boot tape on the tape drive.




7831 0281–034                                                                                     3–5
System Start-Up and Recovery Procedures




        4. If you need to change the boot type, enter SET-TYPE TAPE and transmit. If you
           need to change the tape device name, enter SET-TYPE device-name and
           transmit.
        5.   If jump keys 4 (library load) and 13 (initialization) are not set, enter SJ 4, 13 to set
             them, and transmit. Enter destroy files to set the reinitialize option, and transmit.
        6. Enter SP to start the partition. The Start Partition (SP) command initializes the
           partition and performs an initial program load of the partition.
        7.   During initialization, the MODIFY CONFIG console message gives you a chance to
             modify the configuration before the system starts running.

3.2. Recovery Boot Procedures
        You can perform a recovery boot from tape or mass storage, and you can initiate it
        either manually or automatically.

            Manual recovery
             Manual recovery never initializes the mass storage file area except when the file
             description on a particular device is destroyed. You can reload the libraries if the
             recovery boot is from tape. You can write a dump of main storage to mass
             storage on a mass storage recovery.
            Automatic recovery
             If the system has automatic recovery enabled (jump key 3 is clear), it can
             automatically recover from most errors.
             Both hardware and software can cause a recovery boot to be automatically
             initiated, which is similar to the manual mass storage recovery boot. A dump of
             main storage can be written to mass storage and saved for analysis.

        The process of recovering a machine following a system hang, system stop, hardware
        autorecovery (on some systems), $!, and so on, is governed by the jump keys set in
        the partition at the time the problem occurs. If a system stop, $!, or hardware
        autorecovery occurs and jump key 3 is clear, the system attempts to recover itself.
        The only operator intervention is to respond to messages that the system displays
        during the recovery phase. The $! keyin is described in Section 11.

        If you have to deal with a system hang or you manually recover the system, then more
        intervention is required. Many of those actions depend on the state of the machine at
        the time of the stop and the state of the machine upon recovery. In some cases, you
        might need to stop autorecovery because the system cannot save the dump and you
        need to save the dump to tape or mass storage.




3–6                                                                                  7831 0281–034
                                             System Start-Up and Recovery Procedures




         When a system error occurs in the Exec, autorecovery reloads the Exec without
         destroying mass storage files. During autorecovery, the console displays messages to
         inform you

            Whether a system dump was taken
            The type of boot and session number
            The date and time of recovery

         For more information on performing and processing system dumps, see Section 6.

         For additional information on recovery concepts, see the Integrated Recovery
         Conceptual Overview (7830 8186) and the Integrated Recovery Utility Operations
         Guide (7830 8194).

System Start-Up and Recovery Procedures

Section 4
General System Operations

         This section covers the following general system operation procedures as they relate
         to the Exec:

            Monitoring system status
            Controlling job flow
            Controlling system components
            Logging system events
            Control unit operation
            Controlling symbiont devices and files
            Manually terminating and recovering the ASIS run

         See Sections 5 through 9 for additional operations procedures.


4.1. Monitoring System Status
         System status is continuously monitored by the Exec and is displayed by four lines at
         the top of the system console. These four lines of information are actually displayed
         as two lines in a rotating sequence. Each sequence is updated twice (6 seconds apart)
         before the other sequence appears.

         The appearance of these lines for 2200/XPA systems is shown in Figure 4-1 and
         Figure 4-2. See the SS keyin description for the 2200/XPA continuous console display
         and for explanations of the continuous console display parameters.

         The following keyins let you monitor other aspects of system operation:

            The BL keyin displays the run-IDs of all runs in backlog. Included in the information
             provided by this keyin are the number of runs in backlog and the number of runs
             that are candidates for opening.
            The EU keyin displays EXPOOL use. Information provided includes the current size
             of EXPOOL, the current amount of EXPOOL in use, and other parameters.
            The FF keyin displays information about files. The keyin is very useful to determine
             which user has a file assigned when a hold condition occurs. The information
             returned includes the name of the common name section that has the file
             assigned, the run-ID or TIP run that has the file assigned, and the host system that
             has the file assigned, if applicable.



7831 0281–034                                                                                  4–1
General System Operations




            IP: BAT xx% DEM xx% R/T xx% TIP xx% IDLE xx%
            OPEN: BAT nnnn DEM nnnn UNOPND: nnnn HOLDS holds




              Figure 4–1. System Status Information, Initial Two Lines



            PAGE IO/M: nnnn MASS: nnnnnnnn KWMEM: nnnnnnn yyyy-dd-mm hh:mm:ss zone
            EXP: xx% - nnnnn E11: nnn ID: level TIP: fnnnnnn/r Q: nnnn A:nnnn




             Figure 4–2. System Status Information, Second Two Lines

            The FS keyin displays the status of components, interfaces, or other partitionable
             devices. The keyin provides information such as its current state (up, down,
             reserved, suspended), its availability, assignments, and other parameters relevant
             to the device.
            The HU keyin displays a list of up to 10 run-ids and the percentage of total
             available IP SUPs each of those run-IDs accumulated within 1 second after entry of
             the HU keyin. It also includes the statistics for application level test and set
             queued activities.
            The MS keyin displays the number of mass storage tracks currently available in the
             up and suspended states for fixed and removable mass storage devices.
            The MU keyin displays central complex memory use. The information is provided
             on a global basis, for a specific run, or for all runs, and is displayed by category (for
             example, Resident Exec and Down Memory).
            The RC keyin displays detailed information on backlogged runs, active runs, and
             runs terminated with output files remaining.
            The SS keyin displays expanded information describing system status. The
             information provided by an SS keyin includes the continuous display information,
             as well as information such as the number of read-and-reply messages sent to all
             consoles, the number of unopened runs waiting for start time, and the number of
             runs being held by the CS H run-ID keyin.
            The T keyin displays information about active demand runs, active batch runs, and
             runs that are being held. Information includes the name of the run and the run
             state.

        Refer to the keyin modules in Section 11 for a complete description of each keyin and
        its operation.




4–2                                                                                   7831 0281–034
                                                                  General System Operations




4.2. Job Control
         Job control is one of the main functions of computer operations. It involves controlling
         jobs according to site requirements, user requests, and system performance. Your
         site requirements are typically set up when the system is configured. User requests
         occur when a run hangs or otherwise does not complete normally. For maximum
         system performance, you need to control the mix of jobs that enter the system,
         remove jobs that demand too many system resources during peak loads, and perform
         other control operations.

         The following keyins let you control jobs in the system:

            The B keyin sets the maximum number of open batch runs allowed in the system.
            The CS keyin sets and releases various system- and run-specific hold conditions.
             Hold conditions include all runs in backlog (master hold), specific runs in backlog,
             demand scheduling runs, and demand terminals.
            The DISPLVL keyin changes the switching priority of batch or demand jobs. The
             keyin can be used to change the switching priority of a single job or multiple jobs.
             Jobs can be selected by categories such as account number, project-ID, user-ID,
             run-ID, and type (batch, deadline, or demand). Once the new priority has been
             activated, it can be reset to the site’s normal priority using another format of the
             keyin.
             All activities in the Exec are associated with switching levels (defined during
             system configuration) that define the activity’s instruction processor switching
             priority.
            The E keyin terminates a run in error. If the keyin specifies a batch run, the run
             terminates with an error and allows a postmortem dump (PMD). If the keyin
             specifies a demand run, the current task is terminated, but the run continues with
             the next control image. The N option of the E keyin prevents the PMD for both the
             batch run and the current demand task that were terminated. The E keyin is
             preferred to the X keyin to terminate runs because it lets shared software
             subsystems and common banks (especially Checkpoint/Restart) remove the run
             from internal tables.
             Note that after an E keyin terminates the current task, a termination task can then
             start up. The dynamic configuration parameter run_termination_task being
             nonblank enables the running of the termination task.
            The RM keyin removes a run from the system that is in the backlog queue. The run
             is removed without regard for any console hold or start time that is in effect on
             the run. When the run is removed, it is opened and then terminates in error.
            The SP keyin suspends a run and the RE keyin resumes a run that has been
             suspended. Jobs that require a long time to complete or are resource-intensive
             are good candidates for suspension, keeping them off the system during peak
             times and letting them back on during off-peak times. When the suspended run is
             resumed, it resumes where it left off. The keyin is also useful when a job gets
             hung in the IP and cannot be terminated with an X or E keyin. Using the SP keyin,
             you can suspend the run until an off-peak time, when a system halt affects fewer
             users and a dump can be issued.



7831 0281–034                                                                                   4–3
General System Operations




           The ST keyin starts a runstream that is stored in a file or element that can be
            scheduled and executed as an independent run. Also, use this keyin to start
            runstreams for other software products.
           The X keyin terminates a run if the E keyin does not terminate the run. Using the X
            keyin on batch runs does not allow a postmortem dump; on a demand run, the
            keyin terminates the current task, but continues on the next control image. The X
            keyin does not notify shared software subsystems or common banks, and
            eventually new checkpoints or restarts cannot be processed because they are
            queued.
            Note that after the X keyin terminates the current task, a termination task can then
            start up. The dynamic configuration parameter run_termination_task being
            nonblank enables the running of the termination task.

        Refer to the keyin modules in Section 11 for a complete description of each keyin and
        its operation.


4.3. Controlling System Components
        Status and availability are the attributes of a system component that determine
        whether you can use it. A component can be up, but not available, because something
        in all paths to the component is down. Keyins are used to bring devices down (DN
        keyin), reserve them for future use (RV keyin), and bring them up (UP keyin), as well as
        modify (MD keyin) and remove (RD keyin) disk packs.

        If an attempt to bring a component online fails, the UP keyin aborts, and you must
        correct the failure in order to use the component. If an attempt to take a component
        offline fails, the component is marked down, and you are warned that the component
        is down but online.

        These keyins let you control system hardware status:

           The DN keyin brings down a component or interface. This keyin is most commonly
            used to bring down a component that is not operating properly for repairs or
            diagnostics. The keyin applies to a component or interface that has an up (UP) or
            reserved (RV) status, and any disk device that has a suspended (SU) status.
           The RV keyin reserves a component or a block of main storage.
           The SU keyin suspends a disk device from being allocated.
           The UD keyin establishes a unit-duplexing environment.
           The UP keyin brings a component or interface up (online). This keyin is most
            commonly used to bring up a component that was taken down because it was not
            operating properly. The keyin works with a component or interface that has a
            down (DN) or reserved (RV) status, any disk device that has a suspended (SU)
            status, and any printer that has been locked out or suspended with an SM device
            L keyin.
           The $! keyin stops the system.

        Refer to the keyin modules in Section 11 for a complete description of each keyin and
        its operation.


4–4                                                                               7831 0281–034

         consequences of using the facility control keyins on different types of components
         and devices.

         The following symbols are used to explain the changes for each device.

          –               Keyin not allowed, not applicable, or not available.

          Sc              Keyin forces system stop when assignments exist; otherwise, status changes
                          immediately.
          C               Status changes immediately.

        Control units are added to or removed from a partition by the Exec during UP or DN
