4.4. Logging System Events
        Use the LG keyin to make text entries in the system log file. The system log file
        contains information about such things as run starting and completion times, console
        messages, checkpoints, device assignments and releases, and hardware fault
        information.

        Your site administrator must advise you of the type of system events that must be
        logged. Refer to the LG keyin module in Section 11 for detailed information about the
        keyin. For more information about the system log file, refer to the System Log
        Operations and Support Reference Manual Volume 1 (7831 0315).




4–6                                                                                   7831 0281–034
                                                                  General System Operations




4.5. Controlling Symbiont Queues and Files
         Use the SM keyin to display the status of symbiont devices and control symbiont
         operation. Symbiont devices include card readers, card punches, printers, and remote
         terminals.

         These keyins are related to symbiont files:

            The SR keyin restores symbiont files that were previously saved to mass storage
             so you can queue them for printing (or punching).
            The SV keyin saves queued print and punch files to magnetic tape.
            The SX keyin deletes queued print and punch files from the system.
            The SQ keyin displays the status of the print files in the print queue.

         Refer to the keyin modules in Section 11 for a complete description of each keyin and
         its operation.


4.6. Manually Terminating and Recovering the
     ASIS Run
         The ASIS (Authentication and Session Initiation Subsystem) background run normally
         has the run-id ASIS. Normally, the background run automatically restarts on every Exec
         recovery boot; however, SOLAR can install ASIS in a noauto mode, which prevents it
         from automatically restarting. Under some circumstances, an operator can have to kill
         the ASIS run, and manual recovery of the ASIS run can also be required in some
         circumstances. Always consult with the appropriate system administrator or security
         officer before performing either of these procedures.

4.6.1. Manually Terminating the ASIS Run
         The normal method of shutting down the ASIS subsystem is with the ASISUT
         SHUTDOWN command; see the User Authentication Administration Guide
         (7850 4586) for more information about this command. If the administrator is unable to
         access the ASISUT, or the ASISUT SHUTDOWN command fails to work, then manual
         termination is required.

         Always try the E keyin first to manually terminate the ASIS background run (E ASIS),
         and use the E keyin against any administrative activities that are processing within
         ASIS. This normally causes ASIS to perform a normal shutdown and to deactivate
         itself, with a dump that reflects the E keyin as the cause of the failure.

         If the E keyin fails to deactivate the ASIS background run, the system administrator
         might want to try to deactivate ASIS with SOLAR’s DEACT processor before
         proceeding to the last resort of using the X keyin.




7831 0281–034                                                                                   4–7
General System Operations




        The X keyin stops the ASIS subsystem from running, but does not deactivate it. The X
        keyin displays a warning message on the console indicating that this action affects the
        ASIS subsystem and asking the operator for permission to proceed. Because this
        action does not deactivate the ASIS subsystem, the Exec soon displays a message on
        the console reporting that ASIS is hung and asking if ASIS must be deregistered.

        Always respond YES to the prompt to deregister ASIS. (This procedure also applies if
        the administrator used SOLAR’s DEACT processor.)

        Always try the E keyin first against any administrative activities that are processing
        within ASIS. If the E keyin fails to terminate the activities, then try the X keyin against
        them.

4.6.2. Manually Recovering the ASIS Run
        The only time manual recovery of the ASIS background run is required is after an ASIS
        shutdown if the site wishes to restart ASIS authentications during the same Exec
        session. Use the ST keyin as follows to restart ASIS (assuming the run-ID is ASIS):

            ST,/Z SYS$LIB$*RUN$.ASIS,[setc],,acct-id/ASIS

        The ASIS background run is unable to distinguish between the following two error
        conditions and thus displays a message on the console stating that the ASIS is already
        initialized and asking whether it must be deactivated:

            Two or more background runs are inadvertently started; in this case, only the first
             one initializes the ASIS and is used for authentications.
            The background run was terminated with the X keyin, but the ASIS was never
             deactivated.

        If possible, contact the system administrator before responding to the message about
        deactivating the ASIS. Otherwise, respond NO to the message if the cause is the first
        condition above (ASIS is up and running again) and respond YES if the cause is the
        second condition above (ASIS was terminated with the X keyin).

        When the response to the message about deactivating the ASIS is NO, the
        background runs FIN.

        When the response to the message about deactivating the ASIS is YES, the
        background run attempts to deactivate the ASIS. If the attempt fails, the background
        run displays a message to that effect on the console and then runs FIN. If the attempt
        to deactivate the ASIS succeeds, the background run displays a message on the
        console that indicates success and asks if the ASIS must be restarted. Respond YES
        to start normal initialization of the ASIS. (Responding NO causes the background run to
        FIN.)




4–8                                                                                  7831 0281–034
Section 5
Tape and Disk Operations

         This section presents operations information related to tapes and disks.


5.1. Tape and Tape Drive Operations
         This section provides information about Tape Automatic Volume Recognition (TAVR),
         cartridge tape subsystems, and the Virtual Tape Handler (VTH).

         TAVR provides great flexibility in mounting tape reels by allowing operators to
         premount tapes before user assignment and by allowing any compatible drive to be
         used to mount a tape after user assignment.

         Half-inch cartridge tape subsystems, such as the 4780, OST4890, OST5136, and
         CTS5236, have many features that enhance system performance and reliability. This
         section contains information on how to use these tape subsystems in an auditing
         environment, and includes an explanation of how the devices operate, operational
         considerations, and error recovery implications for auditing with buffering on.

         The DLT 7000 is a half-inch cartridge tape subsystem, but it cannot be used as an audit
         trail device. It is supported as a boot device and system dump device. You must also
         note that the operator clean message to the system console is not supported on the
         DLT 7000. An LED on the device indicates when the drive needs cleaning or that a bad
         tape has been loaded. Because of a DLT 7000 hardware limitation, the LED display is
         not supported. Therefore, the Exec does not display either the system-ID or the
         reel-ID on the drive.

         The Fibre SCSI tape subsystem (for example, CTS9840) implements Persistent
         Reservation commands to exclusively acquire a tape unit. To release a current
         reservation, the unit must be released by the host currently reserving it by using the
         customary DN keyin.

         A Fibre SCSI unit holding an unwanted reservation can be cleared by use of the
         existing TU keyin or by powering it down. Both methods, however, force another host
         to lose exclusive use of the device as explained in the following bulleted paragraph on
         the TU keyin. An important difference of the Fibre SCSI device is that channel
         initialization does not release the persistent reservation as stated in the TU
         explanation on exclusive use.




7831 0281–034                                                                                 5–1
Tape and Disk Operations




         The following keyins let you control tape drives and handle tape labels:

            Use the TU keyin to release a tape unit from exclusive use. The keyin must be
             used only when the host that has exclusive use is down and is unable to perform
             channel initialization. If channel initialization has been performed, then other hosts
             in the tape unit environment can acquire the devices exclusively without using the
             TU keyin. This keyin unconditionally releases the unit and can force another host to
             lose exclusive use of the device.
            The unload (UL) keyin rewinds the unit or the units of the control unit (with
             interlock), and clears the reel-ID from the unit status tables. To use this keyin, the
             unit must be up (UP) or reserved (RV). The keyin does not cause a change in the
             status of a unit, but makes a unit containing a premounted reel available to you for
             a subsequent reel mount. Use this keyin instead of manually rewinding and
             removing a premounted reel at the tape unit. It ensures that the Exec is aware that
             the reel is no longer premounted. Also use the keyin in these circumstances.
                 When you need to unload premounted reels that you expect cannot be
                  assigned in the near future.
                 When a job terminates in error before all anticipated reels have been used.
                 When the run scheduler changes jobs to a later time.
            The premount (PM) keyin designates a unit as premount-only. When a unit is
             designated as premount-only, it can only be used to premount tapes, or to handle
             tapes that are in the facilities pool of the user that is requesting the tape. An
             absolute device/control unit assignment overrides the premount-only designation.
            The Premount Reset (PR) keyin removes a premount-only designation and can be
             required if runs are waiting for tape facilities. Entering this keyin allows a run that
             is waiting for a tape unit to be assigned one.
            The P option on the Facilities Status (FS) keyin lets you see which units are
             designated as premount-only units.

         Refer to the keyin modules in Section 11 for a complete description of each keyin and
         its operation.

5.1.1.   Compatibility Issues of 18- and 36-Track Tapes and Tape
         Drives
         The following compatibility issues with regard to 18- and 36-track tapes and tape
         drives are important to keep in mind to protect tapes, tape drives, and data integrity.

            Although a tape written on an 18-track cartridge drive can be read on a 36-track
             cartridge drive, a tape written on a 36-track cartridge drive cannot be read on an
             18-track cartridge drive.
            A labeled cartridge tape written in 36-track format appears as an unlabeled
             cartridge tape when mounted on an 18-track cartridge drive. If you answer N to
             the following message, the tape is rewritten without being checked for a label by
             the Exec:
                 Is Tape Labeled? Y,N




5–2                                                                                  7831 0281–034
                                                                    Tape and Disk Operations




            The 36-track cartridge drives support the use of E-cart cartridge tapes, which are
             twice as long as standard cartridge tapes. Standard tapes can be mounted on
             either 18- or 36-track cartridge drives and written in either 18- or 36-track format.
             When using E-cart tapes, however, be aware of the following restrictions:
                  E-cart cartridge tapes must not be mounted on 18-track cartridge drives. This
                   could damage the tape cartridge or the tape drive.
                  E-cart tapes can be written only in 36-track format.


                                                 Cautions
                 A labeled cartridge tape written in 36-track format appears as an unlabeled
                 cartridge tape when mounted on an 18-track cartridge drive. If you answer
                 N to the "Is Tape Labeled? Y,N" message, the tape is rewritten without
                 being checked for a label by the Exec.
                 Do not mount E-cart cartridge tapes on 18-track cartridge drives. This can
                 damage the tape cartridge or the tape drive.



5.1.2.    Compatibility Issues of Low-Density (9840A-B) and
          High-Density (9840C) Tapes and Tape Drives
         The following compatibility issues with regard to low-density and high-density tapes
         and tape drives are important to keep in mind to protect tapes, tape drives, and data
         integrity:

            Although a tape written on a low-density drive can be read on a high-density drive,
             a tape written on a high-density drive cannot be read on a low-density drive.
            A tape written on a low-density drive can be rewritten on a high-density drive,
             assuming the write starts at the Beginning Of Tape. However, once past the
             Beginning Of Tape, new data cannot be appended to the data written by the
             low-density drive.

5.1.3. Compatibility Issues of EDRC and Unisys Proprietary
       Compression
         The following list summarizes the compression issues between enhanced data
         recording capability (EDRC) and Unisys proprietary compression.

            Devices with EDRC compression cannot read tapes made with the Unisys
             proprietary compression.
            Devices with Unisys proprietary compression cannot read tapes made with the
             EDRC compression.




7831 0281–034                                                                                   5–3
Tape and Disk Operations




5.1.4. Working with a TAVR System
        TAVR simplifies the interfaces between a user requesting a tape, the operating
        system, and the system operator to provide more flexibility in mounting tape reels.

        The principal advantages of TAVR are

           You can select the unit on which to mount the reel either before (PREMOUNT) or
            after (AFTERMOUNT) the load message is displayed. This lets you choose where
            and when to mount tapes.
           TAVR eliminates the relationship between the tape file assigned and the unit
            selected, and uses reel-IDs to supervise and control the assignment and release
            of tape reels.

TAVR Terminology
        You must know the following TAVR terms:

        Actually assigned
            means that when a unit is actually assigned, aftermounting of the requested reel is
            not allowed.

        Aftermount
            means that you override the unit specified in the LOAD message and mount the
            reel on any unassigned unit that meets the assignment criteria.

        Available unit
            is an unassigned unit that is up (UP) or reserved (RV) and is compatible with the
            type of unit requested by the user.

        Compatible
            means that the unit equipment code is contained in the CONV selection sequence
            for the requested assign mnemonic. Also, the modes and translator/data converter
            are valid for the assign request. The unit must be within the user quota limits.

        Interlock
            means that a tape has been rewound and unloaded. This tape cannot be used until
            another LOAD message is issued for it and it is reloaded.

        Labeled tape
            is a tape that has a VOL1 label as the first block of data.

        LOAD message
            is a console message requesting that you mount a reel on a specific unit. When it
            is issued with a corresponding LOAD ANY message, you can override the unit
            selected in the LOAD message by mounting the requested reel on one of the
            compatible units in the LOAD ANY message.



5–4                                                                              7831 0281–034
                                                                     Tape and Disk Operations




         LOAD ANY message
            is a console message telling you the device types that are compatible with the
            user’s request.

         Premount
            means that you mount a tape on a compatible unit before a request for the tape is
            processed. The reel-ID of the tape is now known to the Exec (you can display it by
            using the FS keyin).

         Reel-ID conflict
            means that the requested tape reel is being used by another run or another
            activity of the run that requested it. There are two basic conflict times: during file
            assignment and during tape swapping or initializing.

            During file assignment

               If the reel is being used by another run, the request is put in the facilities hold
                queue until the other run finishes with it.
               If the reel is being used by this run and it is a single activity run, the request is
                rejected.
               If the reel is being used by this run and it is a multiactivity run, the request is
                put in the facilities hold queue until it is available.

            During tape swapping or initializing, if the next reel is in use by another run or
            another activity in this run (multiactivity runs), the request is held in the reel-ID
            conflict queue.

         SCRTCH tape
            is a tape that is assigned to a user for the duration of the assignment and then
            made available to the system again for the next SCRTCH tape request. SCRTCH
            tapes have the following conditions:
            Note: The industry use of the word "scratch tape" is different from the Unisys
            term: SCRTCH. To scratch a tape means to make the tape available for anyone
            else to use, that is, scratch it off a personal list of tapes.
               The tapes must be assigned with the reel-ID SCRTCH for the Exec to consider
                them SCRTCH tapes.
               The tape is rewound to the load point and reused when initialization or
                swapping of SCRTCH tape occurs. This rewrites tape 1 for both one-unit
                assignments and two-unit assignments when an attempt is made to swap
                beyond the first reel and the second reel.
               A tape TSWAP function 2 request is not allowed for SCRTCH tapes.
               SCRTCH tapes are not allowed for blank tape assignments.
               SCRTCH tapes are not allowed for a cataloged file.
               Reel substitution is not allowed (other than SCRTCH assignment).




7831 0281–034                                                                                       5–5
Tape and Disk Operations




              SCRTCH tapes are rewound with interlock only when the tapes are freed. The
               tapes are rewound to the load point and reused when they are initialized or
               swapped.
              SCRTCH tapes can be premounted if they are labeled.
               Tape labeling system (TLS) checks only the VOL1 header label.
               You cannot use reel-ID SCRTCH in response to either of the two possible
               ANSWER WITH messages for unlabeled tapes. The ANSWER WITH messages
               are
                   ANSWER WITH REEL NO. ON devnam
                   ANSWER WITH REEL NO. ON devnam E, REEL

              The following types of assign requests are specific assignments of a tape unit
               (the tape unit is also considered to be assigned):
                   An assign request from a privileged account run (a run executing under the
                    tape labeling privileged account)
                   An absolute device, control unit, or logical control unit assign request
                   An assign request with translator/data converter specifications
               Note: If an @MODE facility request or a set-mode I/O request specifies a
               translator or data converter, or asks for block numbering to be turned on,
               the unit is specifically assigned (see Actually assigned in preceding text).

        Tape labeling system (TLS)
           is the area of the Exec that handles aspects of tape labeling, for example, tape
           label creation and tape label verification.

        Temporarily assigned
           means that a unit is tentatively assigned and the operator might mount the
           requested tape on any compatible unit (aftermount); see Actually assigned
           (defined earlier).

        Unlabeled tape
           is a tape that does not have a VOL1 label as the first block of data.

        User pool unit
           is a tape that was assigned by the user and subsequently freed by using the S
           option. The unit is part of the user facility pool until it is assigned again and
           subsequently freed without using the S option.




5–6                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                                                   Tape and Disk Operations




Premount Procedures for TAVR
         Premounting is the process of mounting a reel on an unassigned unit in the up (UP) or
         reserved (RV) state before the reel is requested by a tape assignment, initialization, or
         swap.

         When the assignment request is processed, the following events occur:

         1.    Tape assign processing checks to see whether the tape is already premounted on
               a compatible unit. It does this by assigning processing checks:
                  If the tape is premounted, the assignment is completed without soliciting
                   (with LOAD messages) for intervention.
                  If the tape is premounted on an incompatible tape unit, it is rewound with
                   interlock and a LOAD message is sent that requests mounting the reel on a
                   compatible unit. You can mount the tape on any device type that TAVR has
                   specified.
         2.    At tape initialization or swap time (TINTL/TSWAP requests), the Exec recognizes
               that the requested reel is premounted on a compatible unit and completes the
               user’s request without operator intervention.
         3.    TAVR ensures that reel-IDs are unique within the system. Thus, you cannot
               premount the same reel-ID twice.

Aftermount Procedures for TAVR
         Aftermounting is the process of overriding the unit specified in the LOAD message
         after the assign request has been processed. Aftermounting applies to any request
         except ones in which a specific device type is requested.

         When the Exec processes the assign request, if the requested tape is not premounted
         on a compatible unit, the Exec selects the most compatible tape drive available and
         requests that you mount the tape. The Exec also informs you of other devices the
         tape can be mounted on. You can then override the tape unit that the Exec has
         selected.

         If aftermount is allowed, the Exec sends a LOAD ANY message to you in addition to
         the LOAD message. The formats of the messages are

              LOAD reel-id devnam [RING|NORING] filename - index run-id
              (LOAD reel-id) (ANY UNASSIGNED dev-type1 [,dev-type2,...] )
              (LOAD reel-id) (ANY UNASSIGNED cuname [,cuname,...] )




7831 0281–034                                                                                   5–7
Tape and Disk Operations




Special Considerations for TAVR
        When using TAVR, consider the following:

           Users need to know that reel-IDs must be specified on @ASG statements. This
            lets you premount or aftermount the requested reels. If a tape is assigned without
            specifying a reel-ID, TAVR assumes there are no premounted units for the
            request.
           Each tape must have a unique reel-ID. All reel-IDs are unique, except for SCRTCH,
            BLANK, or reel-IDs specified by using the B option on the @ASG statement. With
            the B option, the Exec does not check for assigned reel-IDs.
           To use TAVR most efficiently, all tapes must be labeled. You can premount
            unlabeled tapes, but increased intervention is required because you can be
            solicited for a reel-ID for the tape.
           TAVR does not let you premount tapes on noninterrupting units.
           For SCRTCH tapes: You can use the SCRTCH reel-ID many times within a run or
            runs. The tape labeling system (TLS) does not check tape file header labels for
            SCRTCH tapes (except for the VOL1 volume header label). The tape is rewound to
            the load point and reused when initialization or swapping of a SCRTCH tape
            occurs.
           If the TAVR feature is installed, the media-ID automatically updates a load time for
            labeled tapes. The media-ID for a SCRTCH tape is not updated until access has
            been made to that tape.
           Reel number substitution is not allowed with TAVR, except for tape file
            assignments that are considered TAVR-specific requests, such as
               Absolute device assignments
               Control unit or logical control unit assignments
               Assignments specifying the reel-ID SCRTCH
               Assignments with data translation/conversion specifications
               Tape file assignments for runs executing under the tape labeling privileged
                account
            The recommended method for reel-ID substitution on a tape file assignment is to
            use an absolute device, control unit, or logical control unit assignment.
           If you premount or aftermount an 18-track tape on a 36-track drive and the user
            has assigned an 18-track device or if you premount or aftermount a 36-track tape
            on an 18-track drive and the user has assigned a 36-track device, an error message
            "Reel reelid cannot be mounted on devicename" is displayed and you have to
            remount the tape on an appropriate tape drive before continuing. See warnings in
            Section 5.1.1.




5–8                                                                              7831 0281–034
                                                                  Tape and Disk Operations




Special Considerations When Using TAVR with a CTL
         When using a Cartridge Tape Library (CTL), consider the following:

            Using TAVR with a CTL allows tape swaps between drives belonging to Cartridge
             Library Units (CLU) and freestanding (non-CLU) drives. For example: A user assigns
             drive A and cartridge reel A in CLU A. The user swaps to drive B and cartridge reel
             B in CLU B. The tape drive in CLU A is released, a new tape drive is assigned to the
             user (drive B), and reel B is mounted on drive B from CLU B. The user then swaps
             to reel C, which does not reside in any CLU. Tape drive B is released and a
             freestanding drive, drive C, is assigned to the user. An operator LOAD message is
             then issued, and you mount reel C on the freestanding drive (drive C).
            To allow the assignment or swapping of cartridge tapes between two different
             CLUs or between a CLU and a freestanding device, two drives are necessary for
             tape drive switching. Consequently, tape drive contentions are more likely to
             occur than if tape assignments and swaps were confined to a single CLU. You can
             use one of the following procedures to avoid or resolve contention among runs
             that are trying to switch each other’s tape drives:
                When you see the message
                 run-id NEEDS reel-id CURRENT DEVICE device - CURRENT, HOLD, RETRY?

                 respond with CURRENT if you want to have the drive that is currently assigned
                 by the run to satisfy that run’s request, thus avoiding tape drive contention.
                 You must then mount cartridge reel-ID on the current drive to satisfy the
                 request.
                To make the run wait for a drive to become available, respond with HOLD
                 instead of CURRENT. If you then enter an RC run-ID keyin, the hold condition is
                 indicated by OPEN/FACIL/TSWAP in the run status field. This hold condition
                 indicates the run waits until a compatible tape unit becomes available. If
                 another run is also attempting to select a busy tape unit, a deadly embrace
                 situation can result. You can resolve the deadly embrace by mounting the tape
                 requested by one of the runs on either the drive that is currently assigned by
                 that run or any compatible drive.
                You can also resolve a deadly embrace by entering an E run-ID keyin to cause
                 one of the runs to terminate in error.

5.1.5. Half-Inch Cartridge Tape Subsystems
         Half-inch cartridge tape subsystems have many features that enhance performance
         and reliability. This subsection contains background information on how these tape
         subsystems can be used in an auditing environment. It includes an explanation of how
         these devices operate, their operational considerations, and error recovery
         implications for auditing with buffering on.

         Note: Although the DLT 7000 also is a half-inch cartridge tape subsystem, it cannot
         be used as an audit trail device.




7831 0281–034                                                                                5–9
Tape and Disk Operations




        To understand the buffered audit environment of the half-inch cartridge tape
        subsystem, you must also understand the basic operating and performance
        characteristics of the subsystem.

        Note: The hardware extended recovery mode is disabled by the Exec for 4780
        drives used as audit trails to ensure that no I/O errors exceed 6 seconds in error
        recovery. If the attempt to turn off extended recovery mode fails, informational
        messages such as AT n leg device REEL reelid *DXR STATUS:024* are displayed.
        The messages are informational only. The audit trail continues to use the drive and
        tape, but with extended recovery enabled. If a hardware error occurs, it might take
        up to 12 seconds for the Exec to detect a problem and issue the timeout message.
        To avoid this problem, swap the audit trail to another control unit.

Buffering
        The half-inch cartridge tape subsystem operates in buffering-on (BUFON), buffering-off
        (BUFOFF), buffering-file (BUFFIL), or buffering-tape (BUFTAP) mode. BUFFIL is
        supported for CTS5236, CTS9840, CTS9940, T10000, DLT, and LTO tape subsystems.
        BUFTAP is supported for CTS9840, CTS9940, T10000, DLT, and LTO tape subsystems.

        BUFON
            enables buffered write mode for user data blocks. Because user data blocks do
            not have to wait for physical tape motion, the BUFON mode of operation is faster
            than the buffering-off mode.

        BUFOFF
            disables any buffered write mode.

        BUFFIL
            enables buffered write mode for user data blocks and file marks. The system
            ensures that writing of the data to tape occurs at the close of a file. Because
            BUFFIL mode enables buffered write mode for user data blocks and file marks, it
            is faster than BUFON mode.

        BUFTAP
            enables buffered write mode for user data blocks and file marks for the entire
            tape. Because BUFTAP mode enables buffered write mode for user data blocks
            and file marks for the entire volume, it is faster than BUFFIL mode.

        Note: BUFON and BUFOFF both turn off buffering of tape marks.

        BUFON is a user-selectable write mode that can be requested for a tape file on the
        assignment (@ASG) statement or by using an @MODE statement after the file is
        assigned. BUFON is the system default when the configuration tag tape_buffering is 0.




5–10                                                                           7831 0281–034
                                                                    Tape and Disk Operations




         When BUFON is enabled, user data blocks are transferred from the host computer to
         the buffer of the control unit, where they are temporarily stored. The control unit
         releases the channel and notifies the host that the write is complete (this occurs
         before the data is actually written to the tape). Data from several write operations is
         collected in the buffer until the buffer is full or until a short timer expires. Then the
         control unit starts the tape drive and physically writes the data from the buffer to the
         tape.

         In the BUFOFF mode, data is transferred from the host to the control unit to the tape.
         An ending status is not returned to the host until the tape drive is started and the data
         is transferred to the tape. Because the host computer must wait for physical tape
         motion, BUFOFF mode is slower.

         When using a cartridge tape subsystem for audit tapes, you must consider the effects
         of buffering on the integrity of the audit tape. If buffering is turned off (BUFOFF mode
         is selected) when a write is made to tape, status is not returned to the Exec until the
         data is physically on the tape. If a drive or control unit error occurs in those
         circumstances, the integrity of the data can be guaranteed. With buffering turned off,
         however, performance is lower than with buffering on. For example, an audit write to
         a CTS5236 tape with buffering off takes an average of 68 milliseconds to complete, as
         compared to only 3 milliseconds with buffering on.

         BUFFIL in the buffered-write subfield of the @ASG, @CAT, and @MODE statements
         enables tape marks, in addition to labels and data blocks, to be buffered in the tape
         subsystem until a synchronization point is reached. Synchronizing of the buffer to tape
         is done after the last EOF or EOV of a file or volume.

         If BUFFIL is not set, each Write Tape Mark request causes a synchronization. These
         synchronizations can slow performance when many small files are written to a labeled
         tape.

         When using TMB, it is your responsibility to retain the original data for the user to
         perform error recovery, if that is your requirement. The Exec provides no means for
         you to recover this data. With the use of the BUFTAP option, it is possible for you to
         have received good status on previous writes of thousands of files, when in fact none
         of the files made it to tape. If an error occurs while the files are being written to tape,
         the remaining blocks in the tape subsystem buffer cannot be written onto the tape.
         The Block ID SAFE (BSAFE$) I/O function provides a means for the users to determine
         what block has been actually written to tape by the tape subsystem. A FSAFE$ I/O
         function also provides for synchronization of tape, but this impacts tape performance.

         Note: If an SBCON CTS9840 or SBCON CTS5236 is to be reset or powered down,
         you must down (DN) the tape unit first. If you reset or power it down while it is in
         the UP state, and a user assigns a tape with the BUFFIL or BUFTAP option enabled,
         the option causes a command reject with the fault symptom code “1A 1D” in sense
         bytes 10 and 11.




7831 0281–034                                                                                   5–11
Tape and Disk Operations




Buffering Example
        To achieve maximum performance, I/O must be done with buffering on. However,
        writing to the tape with buffering on can jeopardize the integrity of the audit trail, as
        illustrated by the following example.

        In a half-inch cartridge tape subsystem (BUFOFF) environment, a block of data is
        physically written to tape before status is returned to the host. If an error occurs, the
        block is sent again. If the retry is unsuccessful, the audit reel is closed and the block is
        written to the next reel.

        In the tape subsystem (BUFOFF) environment, a block of data is physically written to
        tape before status is returned to the host. If an error occurs, the block is sent again. If
        the retry is unsuccessful, the audit reel is closed and the block is written to the next
        reel.

        To illustrate further, assume the control unit attempts to write block 50 to reel 1, but
        an error occurs. With buffering off, there is no problem. The audit reel is closed and
        block 50 is written to reel 2.

        In the tape subsystem audit environment with buffering on, however, data is written
        to the control unit buffer and later to the tape. Using a similar illustration, the host
        writes block 50 to the buffer and continues to write blocks 51, 52, and 53.

        If an error occurs at this point, before the control unit writes the blocks to tape, the
        host is notified on the next write to the buffer (in this example, block 54).

        The Exec then writes ending system labels to reel 1 and attempts recovery by writing
        block 54 to the next reel. Notice that blocks 50, 51, 52, and 53 are not recovered and
        they are not on the reel 1 or the next reel, reel 2. This leg of the audit trail is now
        missing four audit blocks and cannot be used for recovery purposes.

        Similar problems could happen if one or both control units lose power or have
        catastrophic hardware errors that cause buffer loss.

        To maintain the integrity of the audit trail with buffering on, you must configure dual
        Exec audit trails, place each leg on separate subsystems (with either dual or single
        control unit), and protect each tape subsystem with a reliable Uninterruptible Power
        Source (UPS).


                                                Caution
             Using a single control unit can degrade system performance while a spare
             is being mounted. If only one control unit has access to the primary and
             spare tapes, I/O is locked out to the primary tape for 1 to 2 seconds after
             the spare tape is mounted. This delay reflects the time it takes the drive to
             read the ANSI-standard hardware density identifier and reposition itself for
             use.




5–12                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                                                    Tape and Disk Operations




Audit Trail Legs
         In the buffered audit environment of the half-inch cartridge tape subsystem, the audit
         trail must be opened by using operator keyins which ensure that each leg of the audit
         trail is maintained on a separate subsystem. These keyins are a minor variation of the
         usual keyins used to open the audit and must be documented at your site. An example
         of the keyin and the instructions for keeping the audit legs on separate subsystems
         are given in Section 11. Once the audit is opened on separate subsystems, the Exec
         automatically maintains the separation of the audit trail tapes as long as no errors
         force the Exec to swap off the originally designated subsystem. In that case, you must
         manually swap the audit legs back to their original subsystems. The procedure to
         return the audit trails to the correct subsystem is very simple.

         The only other significant error is a datacheck. A datacheck error can occur when the
         control unit attempts to write a buffered data block to the tape and the write fails due
         to drive or media errors. This error causes the Exec to enter error recovery and swap
         to the next reel, which results in the audit leg missing several blocks of data.

         The simplest solution to this problem is to duplicate the part of the audit leg that did
         not have the datacheck. This approach can be implemented easily with an Executive
         Control Language (ECL) runstream. The runstream requires you to indicate which tape
         was mounted on the unaffected leg of the audit when the error on the damaged leg
         occurred. The runstream duplicates part of the good leg, ensuring that if the audit
         tapes are needed due to a subsequent system failure, two copies of the data
         necessary to recover the system exists. Sites can choose not to use this runstream or
         enhance it to make multiple copies of the good audit leg. A sample runstream is
         presented later in this section.

         Even with multiple backup and fallback capability, it is still possible for multiple error
         conditions to combine and result in simultaneous failure of both audit legs and loss of
         some amount of audit data. These errors are extremely unlikely. The recovery for
         these types of errors requires manual procedures at the site.

         The site needs to perform an IRU long recovery to recover as much of the audit data
         as possible between the two audit legs. After long recovery, the database is usable,
         although some transactions can be lost.

Automatic Cartridge Loader
         The automatic cartridge loader (autoloader) is an optional device that can automatically
         load tapes into the drive one at a time and accepts unloaded tapes into a discharge
         tray.

         The autoloader has three modes of operation:

            Auto Mode
             The autoloader loads a cartridge into the drive whenever the drive is empty; the
             cartridges remain in the loader. No system intervention is necessary on a system
             with Tape Automatic Volume Recognition (TAVR). Each new tape is considered a
             premount. On a system without TAVR, the cartridge is unloaded immediately if no
             outstanding request exists for that tape drive.



7831 0281–034                                                                                  5–13
Tape and Disk Operations




            You can use the autoloader in auto mode to premount a series of cartridges that is
            requested in a given order. As soon as one cartridge is unloaded, the next is
            loaded into the drive and the system considers the cartridge as premounted. This
            method of operation is best used only on drives that have been placed in the
            premount-only state by a PM keyin.
            If this keyin is not used, as with any form of premount on a TAVR system, and if
            any other requestor needs the drive while it is in a premounted state, the
            premounted cartridge is unloaded and the operator is requested to load the
            requested cartridge. This can result in a sequence of messages as the autoloader
            loads each cartridge in the loader and then rejects it as the wrong cartridge. If this
            happens, put the autoloader in either system or manual mode and load the correct
            cartridge.
           Manual Mode
            The autoloader works only when you press the START button and manually load a
            cartridge. Once the cartridge is unloaded, you must repeat the same process.

           System Mode
            Notes:
               System mode is not supported on OST5136, CTS5236 (SCSI or SCSI2W), or
                LTO tape subsystems. System mode is supported for the SBCON version of
                CTS5236.
               For the 4780 tape subsystem to detect system mode, the SYSTEM mode
                indicator must be lit.
            Multivolume file assignments or blank/scratch (BLANK/SCRTCH) pools. Priority is
            given to BLANK/SCRTCH tape assignments when a cartridge is in the autoloader;
            in this case, a drive is selected and a SELECTED message is displayed on the
            console, not a LOAD message. File assignments requesting more than one
            media-ID attempt to use a drive with an empty autoloader. LOAD messages are
            issued, requesting that up to the first 6, 10, or 24 (depending on the type of
            autoloader) cartridges be loaded.
            When a TSWAP$ function 0 or 1 request comes in (see the description of TSWAP$
            in the Exec System Software Executive Requests Programming Reference
            Manual (7830 7899)) and the autoloader contains cartridges, the autoloader loads
            the next cartridge into the drive to determine if the cartridge is usable. (For
            specific requests, the autoloader loads the next cartridge and determines whether
            the cartridge is correct.) When the loaded cartridge is not usable (correct), it is
            unloaded and a LOAD message is issued.

        The autoloader does not give any indication to the software of the mode in which it is
        operating. If discrepancies arise between what is expected and what actually occurs
        on your drives, check the mode setting on the autoloader. Messages concerning the
        state of the autoloader and commands to the autoloader might not work properly in
        auto or manual mode.




5–14                                                                               7831 0281–034
                                                                  Tape and Disk Operations




         The Exec displays three types of messages on the half-inch cartridge tape unit display:

         system-id
              appears when the unit has been brought up (UP) or reserved (RV) and no cartridge
              is requested or mounted. The system-ID is determined by the configuration
              statement IDS IS SITEID ’site-id-name’ or by the partition_name field in the PDB.

         media-id (flashing)
              appears when a request is issued for a cartridge to be mounted on the unit.

              Note: The media-ID continues to flash on the display until the requested
              cartridge is loaded.

         media-id (nonflashing)
              appears when a cartridge is in the tape drive.

         Notes:
             If the TAVR feature is not installed, labeled and scratch tapes cannot be updated
              until access is made to the tapes.
             Any cartridges remaining in the autoloader are assumed to be BLANK by the
              system and are loaded into the drive on a request for a BLANK or SCRATCH
              cartridge. If the cartridges are labeled, this results in the cartridge being
              unloaded, and the operator is requested to load a BLANK cartridge. If the
              cartridges are unlabeled, they can be overwritten by this request.

         When a multivolume file is assigned to a device with an autoloader configured, the
         following messages are issued to the console (the actual number of messages
         depends on the type of autoloader and the number of volumes for the file):

             LOAD 1 vol1/vol2/vol3 devnam [RING|NORING] filename index runid
             LOAD 2 vol4/vol5/vol6 devnam [RING|NORING] filename index runid
             .
             .
             LOAD 8 vol22/vol23/vol24 devnam [RING|NORING] filename index runid
             SERVICE 1 vol1/vol2/vol3 devnam [RING|NORING] filename index runid
             SERVICE 2 vol4/vol5/vol6 devnam [RING|NORING] filename index runid
             .
             .
             SERVICE 8 vol22/vol23/vol24 devnam [RING|NORING] filename index runid

         Note: Cartridges must be loaded into the autoloader in the correct order; if not,
         you require an operator intervention.

Tape and Disk Operations




Information Displayed by Subsystem LEDs
        The LEDs on half-inch cartridge tape subsystems provide status and loading
        information. The LEDs can indicate any of the following:

           Load User-Defined Tape
            When a user requests a specific tape, the media-ID of the requested tape is
            displayed by the LEDs. When the tape is requested, but not yet loaded, the
            media-ID flashes. Once the Exec recognizes the tape has been successfully
            loaded, the media-ID continues to be displayed but it no longer flashes.
           Load Blank Tape
            When a blank tape is requested by a user, the LEDs indicate whether the tape
            must be labeled or unlabeled and if write protection has been specified.
            When a labeled blank tape is requested, the LEDs display one of the following
            messages:
               x-BLNK F if write-protection was specified by the user
               x-BLNK U if write-enable was specified by the user
               x-BLNK if neither write-protection nor write-enable was specified by the user
            where:
            x is an L if the tape must be labeled and a U if the tape must be unlabeled.
           Cleaning Required
            The cartridge tape drives maintain a record of the amount of tape that has passed
            through the read and write heads since the drive was last cleaned. When this
            number reaches a predetermined point and a tape is unloaded from the drive, a
            *CLEAN* message appears on the LED display of most cartridge tape subsystems.
            The *CLEAN* message does not disable use of any of the drives; you can continue
            to mount cartridges and use the drives even if they are not immediately cleaned.
               On 4780, SCSI 5136, SBCON CTS5236, CTS9840, U9840D, U9840C, T10000A,
                and LTO subsystems, the following operator message is displayed on the
                console: XXXX REQUIRES CLEANING, where xxxx is the name of the drive
                requiring cleaning. This operator message is displayed following each unload
                until the drive is cleaned.
               On SCSI 5136 subsystems, the *CLEAN* LED message does not appear while
                the drive is up even if it needs cleaning. The *CLEAN* LED message is shown
                only when the drive is brought down and needs cleaning.
               On SCSI CTS5236 subsystems, no message is sent to the console.
               On CTS5236 subsystems, the *CLEAN* LED message is not supported by the
                subsystem. The indication that cleaning is required differs among the CTS5236
                models; refer to the hardware manual to find out the method of indication.
               On OST4890 subsystems, an operator message is displayed on the console
                and the LED displays "CLEAN ME."
               On the DLT 7000, no message is sent to the operator console. An LED on the
                device lights when the drive needs cleaning.



5–16                                                                             7831 0281–034
                                                                    Tape and Disk Operations




             System-ID
              When no cleaning is required and no tape is loaded or requested, the LEDs display
              the system-ID number.
             Unit number
              If the unit is down, the LEDs display the unit number of the drive.

         Note: Due to hardware limitations, the Exec does not display either the system-ID
         or reel-ID on the DLT 7000 or LTO tape drive.

         Duplex Audit Tape Open Procedure in
         For modern tape devices (that is 9x40s), there is a one-to-one correspondence
         between subsystems and drives. However, for earlier tape device (that is 5136,
         5236s), there were multiple drives for a subsystem. To protect from drive, media and
         subsystem failures for these earlier devices, the duplex audit trail legs need to be
         located on different subsystems.

         To locate the legs of a duplex audit trail on separate subsystems, use the following
         example procedure. The example shows a duplex audit with two spares configured:

             Subsystem 1, audit leg 1
                 Tape CU TCN0,TCN1
                 Active unit TN00
                 Spare unit TN01
             Subsystem 2, audit leg 2
                 Tape CU TCN2,TCN3
                 Active unit TN20
                 Spare unit TN21

         Determine which tapes to use. You need four tape units, two units on each of two
         available subsystems. The objective is to have one primary unit and one spare unit on
         each tape subsystem. Always use the following keyin to open the audits.

             AT 1 OP primary1/primary2,spare1/spare2

         where:

         primary1
              is the primary unit for leg 1 on subsystem 1.

         primary2
              is the primary unit for leg 2 on subsystem 2.

         spare1
              is the spare unit for leg 1 on subsystem 1.



7831 0281–034                                                                              5–17
Tape and Disk Operations




        spare2
             is the spare unit for leg 2 on subsystem 2.

        Using the example configuration, this keyin is

            AT 1 OP TN00/TN20,TN01/TN21

        Units TN01 and TN21 are spares. The Exec always picks a spare unit on the same
        string as the active audit tape. If the active audit tapes ever end up on the same
        subsystem due to error, you have to manually swap one of the active audit tapes to a
        spare on the other subsystem. Once the swap is done, no further action is required.

        Audit Tape Copying
        To recover from drive or media failures, the buffered audit tape backup program on
        the half-inch cartridge tape subsystem must be available on your system at all times.
        This program protects your site from data loss due to failure in one audit leg.

        The program relies on the requirement that, on half-inch cartridge tape subsystems, all
        buffered audit environments be duplex. Duplexing means that at any time there are
        two identical copies of any particular audit record, and this implies that the typical
        duplex environment always has four tape drives dedicated to audit: two active primary
        drives or legs, and two spare drives.

        For example, consider the following duplex audit environment:

            Subsystem 1, audit leg 1
                Tape CU TCN0,TCN1
                Active unit TN00 tape reel C001
                Spare unit TN01 tape reel C002
            Subsystem 2, audit leg 2
                Tape CU TCN2,TCN3
                Active unit TN20 tape reel C003
                Spare unit TN21 tape reel C004

        If an error occurs on TN00, reel C001, it is likely that some of the data that must be on
        reel C001 is missing. The data that is missing on reel C001 is present on reel C003, and
        reel C003 is the only copy of the data. It can be unlikely that problems occur with reel
        C003, but it is necessary to protect the data as much as possible by using the BACKUP
        runstream.

        This runstream protects the audit data by making a duplicate copy of the data on the
        good audit leg (reel C003).




5–18                                                                             7831 0281–034
                                                                      Tape and Disk Operations




         You need to back up an audit tape whenever you suspect an error on the tape has
         caused data to be missing from one of the duplexed legs. These cases include (but are
         not limited to) occurrences of the DATCHK, INTREQ, or EQPCHK I/O error messages;
         or any time an unusual error occurs on the drive. If you are in any doubt about whether
         to back up a tape, it is always best to do the backup.

         To perform the backup, proceed as follows:

         1.    Identify the tape to be backed up. Remember, you are backing up the good leg of
               the audit. Examine the printed output to determine which reel was active on the
               audit trail leg that is free of errors (the good reel) when the error occurred on the
               other leg (the bad reel).
         2.    Determine whether the good reel is still active (mounted); use the AT 1 FS keyin to
               do this. If the tape is still mounted, swap off the reel by using the AT 1 SW keyin.
         3.    If you are not sure which tape was active at the time of the error on the other leg,
               make backup copies of several of the tapes that were active around the time of
               the error. You might encounter this case if the good leg happened to swap to the
               spare at or near the time of the error on the bad leg.

         The following example shows how the BACKUP utility could be used. It also shows a
         typical sequence of system console messages; the reel numbers and drive names
         used are for example only. In addition, the example is annotated to explain the actions
         that can be executed at each step of the program (comments are enclosed in
         parentheses).

              ST qualifier*filename.BACKUP,,,OPUTIL/0

              BACKUP START

              1-BACKUP*ENTER REEL NUMBER OF ... AUDIT TAPE TO COPY OR X TO END

         Reply X to end the program without a backup or enter the reel number of the AUDIT
         tape you want to back up.

              1 C003

              1-BACKUP*ENTER BLANK REEL NUMBER FOR BACKUP REEL.

         Reply with the number of a blank reel you want to become the backup of the AUDIT
         tape previously entered.

              1 C006

              1-BACKUP*ARE YOU SURE REEL C006 IS A BLANK? ACK=YES, X= ABORT

         Reply X if you have entered the incorrect blank reel number; otherwise, transmit to
         continue the program.




7831 0281–034                                                                                   5–19
Tape and Disk Operations




          1 YES

          LOAD C003 TN00 NORING AUDIT$01 -1 BACKUP

        Mount reel C003. Note that this reel must be write-protected (NORING).

          LOAD C006 TN01 RING BACKUPREEL -1 BACKUP

        Mount reel C006. Note that this reel must be write-enabled (RING).

          BACKUP*MSG: BACKUP OF TAPE C003 DONE.

          1-BACKUP *ENTER REEL NUMBER OF AUDIT TAPE TO COPY OR X TO END

        If additional backup copies are needed, enter another blank reel to continue the
        program; otherwise, enter X to end.

          1 X

          BACKUP*TAPE BACKUP DONE.

        In this example, reel C006 is now a backup tape for reel C003 and can be used by
        Integrated Recovery Utility (IRU) during recovery if there is an error on reel C003.

        Remember that the backup reel is needed only if the audit tapes are required for
        recovery and if the original audit tape is unreadable. Although this situation is not likely
        to occur, the following example shows how the backup reel is used. Once again, the
        example is annotated to explain actions to be taken by the operations personnel
        during recovery.

        Note: It is imperative that your system have the tape configuration tag TLSUB
        (reel_nbr_substitution_allowed) set to 1 or TRUE. This allows the substitution of the
        backup reel for the failing recovery tape.

        IRU displays a message with the following format during recovery:

          IRU*7015 Audit trail read error - 012- OPER B REPLY
          IRU* on audit reel C003
          IRU*AT3 Read TBSN: xxx TIME: yy/mm/dd hh:mm:ss zzz
          1-IRU*7234 Enter DEVICE, DUPLEX, SKIP, DUMP, END, or ?

        If the reel just listed has a backup, you can reply as follows:

          1 DEVICE

          LOAD C003 TN00 NORING AUDIT$01 -1 IRU




5–20                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                                                   Tape and Disk Operations




         Do not mount C003; instead, mount the backup reel C006. You can expect to get the
         following message:

           1-TN00 WRONG REEL, LOAD C003 A,E,REEL

         Reply with the number of the backup reel.

           1 C006

           LOAD C006 TN00 NORING AUDIT$01 -1 IRU

         Remount reel C006 if necessary. The IRU now uses reel C006 as a substitute for reel
         C003; this is acceptable because reel C006 is an exact copy of C003.

         Following is an ECL runstream that duplicates a part of the audit leg that did not have
         the deferred datacheck. You must indicate which tape is mounted on the unaffected
         leg of the audit when the error on the damaged leg occurred. The runstream
         duplicates part of the good leg, ensuring that if the audit tapes are needed due to a
         subsequent system failure, two copies of the data necessary to recover the system
         exists.

           @run backup,0,sys$,999,999
           @elt,iq tpf$.sgs,,stop
           * .
           * . This is a sample runstream that can be used as a guide for sites
           * . to make their own audit U47M tape backup run. Most sites will want to
           * . make modifications to accommodate local programming standards.
           * . All sites should test the backup runstream to be sure it works at
           * . their site.
           * .
           * . There must be at least one DEVICE SGS and the first one
           * . will be the only one used; other DEVICE SGSs are ignored.
           * . The DEVICE SGS has the following fields.
           * . Field 1 = the assign mnemonic to use for tape @asg
           * .
           * . sgs
           device hicm
           device u9s
           device t
           * .
           * . The EXPIN and EXPOUT SGSs are used to supply appropriate
           * . expiration fields for the input and output tape assignments.
           * .
           expin 31
           expout 31
           * . Define the name of the audit file.
           * . This is necessary since the Exec assigns each audit tape without
           * . the 'F' option so the file name here must match the file name
           * . the Exec used to create the tape.
           file sys$*audit$01
           * .
           @end stop

          @ssg,ike ,tpf$.sgs,tpf$.addstream,,tpf$.sysstream
          skel
          * . SSG runstream to make a backup tape of a given audit tape.
          * .
          *accept,blo audit 'Enter reel number of original audit tape to copy or "x"
          to end'
          * .
          *if [audit,1,1,1,ucstr$] <>X
          *accept,blo backupreel 'Enter blank reel number for backup reel.'
          * .
          * . Give operator a chance to abort run if input is no good.
          #msg copy original audit reel [audit,1,1,1] to [backupreel,1,1,1]
          #msg,w are you sure reel [backupreel,1,1,1] is a blank? ack=yes, x=abort
          * .
          #asg,tf [file,1,1,1].,[device,1,1,1],[audit,1,1,1],[expin,1,1,1],noring
          * .

          #asg,tf backupreel.,[device,1,1,1],[backupreel,1,1,1],[expout,1,1,1],ring
          * .
          * . Now copy the good reel to the backup reel.
          * .
          copy,m [file,1,1,1].,backupreel.
          #msg backup of tape [audit,1,1,1] done.
          #free [file,1,1,1].

          sgs
          #add,l tpf$.sysstream
          #eof
          #eof
          *else
          *display,o 'runstream terminated by operator X keyin'

          @eof

5.1.6. Virtual Tape Handler
         The virtual tape handler (VTH) simulates tape drives on mass storage, which can be
         fixed or removable packs. A tape assign mnemonic mapped to VTH indicates mass
         storage is simulating that type of tape drive. There is a limit of 90 types of devices that
         can be defined as virtual tape devices at any given time. Files with qualifiers and
         filenames of SYS$*$VTHR$xxxxxx are considered virtual tapes, with xxxxxx
         representing the reel-ID.

         Programs cannot differentiate between a virtual tape and a real tape except on a
         virtual tape

            Fast tape access (FTA) is not supported.
            The capacity of newer cartridge drives is not simulated.
            A program that reads backwards can get a different word returned than from a
             real tape.
            Audit trails are not supported on VTH.
         See the Exec System Software Executive Requests Programming Reference Manual
         (7830 7899) for more specific information about the preceding exceptions.

         An area of mass storage is designated as a pool (SYS$*VTH$POOL) of reel-IDs to be
         used when programs require a blank or scratch tape. When a program no longer
         needs a scratch tape, the reel-ID is returned to the pool and is available for use as a
         blank or scratch tape by another program. The system administrator defines how
         many IDs must be in the pool and has to add more if the pool becomes depleted.

         This pool also identifies the types of devices that can and cannot be defined as virtual
         tape devices. The type of device used for the default system tape must not be
         allowed as a virtual device because this would prevent rollbacks.

5.1.7. DVD Tape
         The DVD tape is a DVD that simulates a read-only tape. You cannot write to it. For
         example, it cannot be an Audit Trail device or Dump device. It is supported as a
         read-only tape and also a boot device. DVD tapes have the same characteristics of
         most open reel types and therefore do not support fast tape access (FTA), block
         numbering, data compression, buffered writes, or expanded buffer. DVD tapes do not
         support ACS or CTL; therefore assignments with CTL-pool are rejected. Because the
         DVD-tape is read-only, the use of the W option, the indication of RING or
         WRITEENABLE in the ring field, and the indication of BLANK or SCRTCH in the reel-ID
         field are all rejected.




7831 0281–034                                                                                 5–23
Tape and Disk Operations




5.2. Disk and Disk Drive Operations
        You must use DPREP to prepare disks for use. See the Exec System Software
        Installation and Configuration Guide (7830 7915) for more information.

        Each disk pack is labeled as either a fixed disk or a removable disk. Before using a disk
        pack, the system reads the label of the pack to identify it as fixed or removable, and to
        make sure that it is correctly prepped. If it is not correctly prepped, the system
        displays a series of messages that you must respond to.

        Once a fixed disk is used by the system, it cannot be removed from the system
        without rebooting the Exec. If a fixed disk is removed from the system, all the files on
        that fixed disk is lost or disabled. To avoid any loss of files, the same fixed disks that
        were used during a system session must be available when a manual or automatic
        recovery is performed.

        During either the initial load or a recovery, the Exec tries to read a label record from
        each disk pack, either online or suspended. If the pack is not prepped or there is no
        label, the system displays I/O error messages on the console. The system displays a
        message that prompts you to modify the configuration. At that time, you can change
        the status of any units in the configuration.

        During an initial load or a recovery, the Exec counts the number of fixed mass storage
        devices and displays this message:

            0-FIXED MS DEVICES=nn-CONTINUE? YN

        If the number, nn, is correct, respond by entering a Y, and the boot continues. If the
        number is not correct, respond by entering an N. After a short delay, the system stops
        with an error. If the system does not display the correct number of devices in the
        message, enter an FS,MS keyin before you answer the message. The keyin produces
        a list of devices found, and you can see which ones are fixed.

        These keyins let you control disk drives and disk packs:

            The DC keyin copies the contents of one disk pack to another disk pack. All disk
             types, except those that are physically removable, are valid. The DC keyin can be
             used for local and shared disks.
            The IN keyin initializes a fixed mass storage device (disk drive). Use caution, since
             this keyin destroys all information currently on the device. Do not use this keyin on
             removable mass storage devices.
            If DN,PACK (down pack) keyins are configured, you can use the MD keyin to place
             inhibits on or to modify the current inhibits of a disk pack in the UP, DOWN, or
             PRESENT state. If you use the MD keyin on a disk pack that does not currently
             exist, the pack is added to the list of packs in the PRESENT state. The benefit of
             adding disk packs in this manner is that it allows you to place inhibits on a pack
             currently not on the system so that when it is put on the system its access is
             restricted as needed. (See 5.2.1 for more information on the down pack keyins.)




5–24                                                                               7831 0281–034
                                                                  Tape and Disk Operations




            The MS keyin displays information about the availability of mass storage on the
             system. The keyin displays, for each device type, the number of mass storage
             tracks currently available.
            The MV keyin moves a fixed disk pack or an assigned removable disk pack from a
             failing drive to another drive. The keyin must be entered before the pack is moved.
             If the disk pack is moved first, I/O errors can occur. The keyin is valid only for
             physically removable devices.
            The RD keyin removes disk packs in the DN or PRESENT state from the pack
             status table to make room for other packs. The maximum number allowed is
             defined in the system configuration. This keyin cannot be used on a unit-duplexed
             device. (See 5.2.1 for more information on the down pack keyins.)
            Use the RP keyin to remove all pointers to files that reside on removable disk
             packs from the STD or SHARED master file directory (MFD).

            The EXPAND keyin expands the internal OS data structures of a pack to make use
             of the larger disk capacities. It can be used to expand local and shared disks.

         Refer to the keyin modules in Section 11 for a complete description of each keyin and
         its operation.

5.2.1. Controlling Removable Disk Packs with Down Pack
         The Down Pack feature lets you bring down or restrict access to a pack prepped as
         removable (without causing a system stop). You can bring down or restrict access to
         the removable pack even though files are assigned on the pack, user I/O requests are
         in progress, or the pack is unmounted.

         Down pack keyins are most valuable in an environment where all files on removable
         packs are TIP duplex files. See the Transaction Processing Administration and
         Operations Reference Manual (7830 7881) for examples on the use of down pack
         keyins when the removable packs contain TIP duplex files.

         Notes:

                 The NEWCON configuration parameters REQNBRPST0 and REQNBRPST1
                  control the availability of the Down Pack feature. See the Exec System
                  Software Installation and Configuration Guide (7830 7915) for more
                  information.

                 When the TIPDUPRSL configuration parameter is TRUE, it will initiate the
                  DN,PACK keyin and suppress I/O messages and provides a forced “G”
                  response to the failing removable pack. See the Exec System Software
                  Installation and Configuration Guide (7830 7915) for more information.




7831 0281–034                                                                                 5–25
Tape and Disk Operations




Down Pack Keyins
        The down pack keyins are as follows:

        DN,PACK
            Changes the status of a removable pack to down (DN).

        UP,PACK
            Changes the status of a removable pack to up (UP).

        FS,PACK
            Reports the status of a removable pack.

        MD (modify disk)
            Restricts read, write, or assign access to a removable pack.

        RD (remove disk)
            Removes a pack from the system pack list.

        See Section 11 for more information on these keyins.

Changing the Status of a Removable Pack
        The status of a removable pack is distinct from the status of the disk device that
        contains it. By manipulating the pack status, you might be able to bring down an
        assigned disk device and make repairs without a system reboot.

        The status of a removable pack can be: UP, DN, PRESENT, or NOT PRESENT.

UP Status
        When they are assigned, removable packs receive an initial status of UP, and access
        to an UP pack is unrestricted.

DN Status
        Removable packs receive a status of down (DN) when a DN,PACK keyin is performed
        on the pack. A down pack is disassociated from the disk device where it resides. Once
        this logical separation occurs, the device status can change without regard to
        assignments on the pack.

        Any attempt to access a file on a down pack is rejected. If the file is TIP duplexed, an
        I/O request receives do-not-read/do-not-write (DNR/DNW) status for the leg on the
        down pack, but the request proceeds on the other file leg. If the file is TIP duplexed
        and the TIPDUPRSL configuration parameter is TRUE, then I/O to the pack is given an
        immediate I/O status 015 without setting the DNR/DNW status for the leg. This
        improves the TIP Duplex recovery time and prevents undesirable I/O console
        messages from flooding the system. If the file is non-TIP or TIP simplex, an I/O
        request fails with an I/O status 015. Files might be cataloged, assigned, and freed on a



5–26                                                                             7831 0281–034
                                                                     Tape and Disk Operations




         pack while it has a status of DN. However, any attempt to expand an existing file on a
         down pack is rejected.

         The Exec is unable to update directory information on a down pack. Therefore, when
         one of the following events occurs, an UP,PACK keyin is not allowed until all files on
         the pack are freed or the system is rebooted:

            A cataloged file is assigned or freed on a down pack.
            An RP keyin is successfully performed on a down pack.
            A down pack is reprepped.

         Note: If the pack contains only TIP files, you might release all file space on the
         pack instead of freeing all files.

PRESENT Status
         Removable packs receive a status of PRESENT when a modify disk (MD) keyin is used
         to restrict access to an unassigned pack (that is, the pack has not yet been placed in
         the system’s list of UP, DN, or PRESENT packs). This status allows restrictions to be
         placed on a pack before it enters the system.

NOT PRESENT Status
         The NOT PRESENT status implies the removable disk is not assigned, has no inhibits
         set, and has not been brought down by the DN,PACK keyin (that is, it was not found by
         the system in the list of UP, DN, or PRESENT packs). By definition, removable packs
         have a NOT PRESENT status if they do not have a status of DN, UP, or PRESENT.

Determining the Status of a Removable Pack
         Use the FS,PACK keyin to display the status, inhibits, assign count, and directory-ID of
         a removable pack. See Section 11 for more information on this keyin.

Restricting Access to a Removable Pack
         Use the MD keyin to establish or clear read (R), write (W), or assign (A) inhibits. Read
         and write inhibits correspond to the DNR and DNW I/O inhibits for TIP files. The
         creation of global DNR/DNW attributes for all files on a pack allows TIP recovery
         procedures to be implemented more easily.

         The following apply to disk pack inhibits:

            Any attempt to assign a file on an assign-inhibited (A) pack is rejected.
            Any attempt to read from a file on a read-inhibited (R) pack is rejected. If the file is
             TIP duplexed, a read request receives DNR status for the file leg on the down
             pack, and the request is redirected to the other file leg. If the file is non-TIP or TIP
             simplex, the request fails with an I/O status 015.




7831 0281–034                                                                                   5–27
Tape and Disk Operations




           Any attempt to write to a file on a write-inhibited (W) pack is rejected. If the file is
            TIP duplexed, a write request receives DNW status for the file leg on the
            write-inhibited pack, but the write proceeds on the other file leg. If the file is
            non-TIP or TIP simplex, the request fails with an I/O status 015.
