                APP n RESTARTED
                run-id: App n short recovery complete

              where run-id is the run-ID of the IRU run.

7.2.4. DOWN Option
         Specify DOWN if the application group does not need to be active yet or if further
         diagnosis of the failure is needed before selecting another Exec option. In processing
         the DOWN option, the system

             Closes the audit trail, if it is open
             Releases queue item storage in memory
             Frees the periodic savefile if it is an Exec file
             Leaves the application group in a down state

         When DOWN is successfully completed, the following message appears at the
         console:

                APP n APPLICATION GROUP DOWN

         After initiating the DOWN option, you can use the SUTIL or INIT option at a later time.
         Before specifying SUTIL or INIT, however, you must perform the AP n UP keyin to
         bring up the application group. Select a recovery or initialization option by answering
         the console message prompt that is produced, or alternatively, includes the recovery
         type on the AP n UP keyin (AP n UP SUTIL or AP n UP INIT). You cannot use PANIC and
         REBUILD recovery because the DOWN option closes the audit trail.




7831 0281–034                                                                                   7–19
Integrated Recovery and System Operation




7.2.5. DEFER Option
        DEFER is a Step Control option you can want to specify in the following situations:

            You want to run a concurrent application group in non-RLP mode (described in the
             Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for Multihost
             Environments (7831 0919)).
            Maintenance needs to be done on the ARF or periodic savefiles.

        The DEFER option is similar to the DOWN option, except it frees the ARF from Step
        Control so the host to which you are switching the application group can access the
        ARF. In processing the DEFER option, the system

            Closes the audit trail, if it is open
            Releases queue item storage in memory
            Frees the ARF from Step Control
             Note: The ARF is not freed from Audit Control until the audit trail is deferred.
             Both Step Control and Audit Control for an application group must be deferred
             before the ARF is freed.
            Leaves the application group in a deferred state

        If the DEFER option is completed successfully, the system displays a message
        indicating the application group is deferred.

        You can use the AP n UP keyin to bring up the deferred application group.


7.3. Local TIP Recovery Boot
        Several local TIP configuration parameters can be changed dynamically but do not take
        effect until TIP is initialized again. Changing the values of these parameters sets a flag
        that generates messages during a local TIP recovery boot indicating that a local TIP
        initialization boot is required.

        The messages offer you a bypass option to override the TIP initialization boot, and
        perform an Exec tape recovery boot.

            TIP CONFIG has changed since last session. local TIP Initialization is
            required.
            To bypass this required local TIP Initialization, perform EXEC tape
            recovery boot.
            Answer: I (INIT TIP) OR D (DOWN TIP for this session):




7–20                                                                              7831 0281–034
Section 8
System Operations and Security

         This section presents the following security-related operations information:

            The procedures for initialization of the system security files during initial and
             recovery boots
            A description of the printout labeling feature
            Security considerations when handling removable disks and tapes


8.1. Security Initial and Recovery Boots
         During an initial boot, the operator is asked the following question:

             n Specify Method to obtain Security Files--Load, Initialize (L,I)

         Choose L to load the security files from a tape or tapes. Choose I to initialize the
         security files. The response applies to all security files. When security files are
         initialized, the header is cleared or created, links are established to the allocatable
         portion, and lookup tables are initialized. With SYS$*SEC@USERID$, user-ids and
         security records are created automatically for the Exec (EXEC8), the overhead user-id,
         and the security officer. With SYS$*ACCOUNT$R1, the system standard account and
         master account are created.

         With Fundamental Security, the system always tries to recover the security files
         during a tape or autorecovery boot. If all security files cannot be recovered, they are
         all initialized with messages indicating that fact.

         With Security Level 1 or higher, the operator is given the choice as to whether to
         recover the files as follows:

             n Specify Method to obtain Security Files--Load, Init, Recover(L,I,R)

         The response applies to all security files.

         If the response is L, the operator is asked to load the SEC,RESTORE tape.




7831 0281–034                                                                                    8–1
System Operations and Security




        If security files are loaded or recovered and the configured security officer does not
        match the security officer in the SEC@USERID$ file, the following message is
        displayed:

            0 - The Security Officer configured in the Exec does not match the file [Recovered | Loaded].


            Use the Configured or File Security Officer? (C, F)


        If any of the security files cannot be assigned, fail integrity checks, or receive I/O
        errors, one of the following messages appear, as applicable:

            Security file, < filename>, is corrupted.
            n Specify Method to obtain Security Files--Load, Initialize (L,I)


            Security file, < filename>, could not be cataloged. Facilities status = xxxxxx.
            n Specify Method to obtain Security Files--Load, Initialize (L,I)


            Security file, < filename>, could not be assigned. Facilities status = xxxxxx.
            n Specify Method to obtain Security Files--Load, Initialize (L,I)


            0 - Security file, < filename>, received I/O error     xx; function = yy; sector addr = zz
            System security may be impacted. Contact your Site Administrator.
            Continue using a bad Security File or Stop the System? (Continue, Stop)


        Note: If you restore ACCOUNT$R1, accumulated file mass-storage quota values
        are incorrect. A recovery boot is required to recalculate the actual values from the
        current catalogued files.


8.2. Security Labeling for Printouts
        Your system can be configured to label printouts with security information. Depending
        on how your system is configured, labels can be printed on every page or only on the
        banner and trailer pages of a printout. These labels (see Figure 8–1) appear at the top
        and bottom of each page. Labels are one to four lines long (up to 225 characters).

        The label contains the mandatory attributes of a print file: the clearance level and the
        compartment set. The security officer (or site administrator) defines the
        compartments and clearance levels for the system and each of its users.

        A clearance level is a numerical classification for objects, which ranges from 0 to 63,
        with 63 being the most restrictive. Clearance levels can also be associated with
        symbolic labels such as unclassified, classified, secret, and top secret. A compartment
        is a group based on interest or category, such as accounting, payroll, or personnel. In
        contrast to clearance levels, compartments are nonhierarchical classifications. A
        printout that has been identified as system high by the security system software is
        labeled with clearance level 63 and compartment set ALL.

        Users might cause misprinting of the label through certain actions. For example, they
        can request a different print band or font or they can use ER APRTCN$ in the middle of
        a print file and change the character set representation. After such changes, the
        system continues printing the original label, possibly obscuring the real attributes.



8–2                                                                                        7831 0281–034
                                                          System Operations and Security




         The printouts also have unique sequence numbers printed on the first and last pages.
         Sequence numbers are randomly generated 3-digit numbers printed on the banner and
         trailer pages of every printout to prevent spoofing. They are created when the print
         queue receives the first print file, and are increased by 1 each time another print file
         enters the queue. This process stops as soon as the print queue is empty. When the
         printer becomes active again, a new random sequence number is generated to start
         the next series of printouts.

         When you are sorting print files to distribute to users, check the sequence numbers at
         the beginning and end of print files to make sure they match. Report any mismatches
         to your security officer. You can make an additional check by comparing printout
         labels on the banner and trailer pages, if labeling is configured for your site.

         Compartment set names longer than 225 characters can be used internally, but cannot
         be printed out. An error message results and the printout is labeled system high
         (clearance level 63 and compartment set ALL). Handling of printouts labeled system
         high depends on local site procedures. For example, such printouts can be distributed
         to the security officer.

         The security system creates a log entry in the system log file to show which files have
         a misleading output label.

         Use the SR and SV keyins (see Section 11) to move labeled print files from system to
         system. These keyins also save the current print queue for a single host. If you plan to
         transport SV tapes, make sure the destination system has the identical security
         environment or labels cannot be handled properly.

         The file’s symbolic clearance level appears first in the label, followed by the
         compartment set. Compartment names are never split between lines, and only ASCII
         characters are used in labels.




7831 0281–034                                                                                8–3
System Operations and Security




        Figure 8-1 shows the labeling on a sample output where the symbolic clearance level
        is SECRET, and the user’s compartment set has the compartments PAYROLL,
        PERSONNEL, and PLANNING.




                            Figure 8–1. Sample Output Label

        The label can appear slightly different if any of the following conditions exists:

           The ALL compartment bit is set, and the label has ALL in place of a list of
            compartments.
           No compartments are associated with the file and the label has NULL in place of a
            list of compartments.
           The label is longer than 225 characters and the label has ALL in place of a list of
            compartments and contains this message:
              The security label is too long to be printed.

            In this case, the system also makes a log entry.




8–4                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                                           System Operations and Security




8.3. Handling Removable Disk Files and Tapes in a
     Security Environment
         In a security system, removable disk files and tapes must be handled properly. Any
         removable object not labeled with a valid security label that contains a clearance level
         and compartment set is defined as nonlabeled. Removable objects are known to the
         system only when the pack is registered or the tape is mounted. They can remain
         nonlabeled until a user references them, possibly after a long time delay. The security
         system has detection mechanisms for nonlabeled removable objects.

         If a removable object is labeled, its label has meaning only within a system’s specific
         security environment. An object’s security level (clearance level and compartment set)
         is interpreted relative to the definitions of the system symbolic clearance levels and
         system compartment set.

         When objects are moved from one system to another, the label can then refer to the
         same attributes, but those attributes can have different meanings on each of the
         systems. Thus, the integrity of the label is endangered.

         The security system defines specific requirements for maintaining security while
         transporting removable objects between systems. When removable objects are
         transported between systems, your security officer must check the definition of their
         original security environment, that is, how the system clearance level and system
         compartment set are defined. If they are different from the definitions on your
         destination system, the labels need to be changed immediately, so that the labels
         reflect the original level and compartment set definitions.




7831 0281–034                                                                                 8–5
System Operations and Security




8–6                              7831 0281–034
Section 9
System Operations in a Multihost
Environment

         This section describes the ways in which a multihost operations environment is
         different from a one-host operations environment. It presents an overview of the
         features as a system operator deals with them, tells which console keyins to use to
         determine the status of and control parts of the multihost environment, and tells
         where to look in this manual or in other documents for detailed information.

         The section covers these topics:

            Types of multihost environments (Section 9.1)
            Hosts and shared devices (Section 9.2)
            Files and file directories (Section 9.3)
            Application groups (Section 9.4)
            Audit trails (Section 9.5)
            TIP File Control, scheduling, and automatic recovery (Section 9.6)
            Automatic Recovery of Components (ARC) and the monitor run (Section 9.7)
            XPC (Section 9.8)
            Shared unit duplexing (Section 9.9)


9.1. Types of Multihost Environments
         There are two basic types of multihost environments, depending on which features
         you have installed on your system

            A Multi-Host File Sharing (MHFS) environment
            An Extended Transaction Capacity (XTC) environment

         MHFS provides the foundation for an XTC environment.




7831 0281–034                                                                              9–1
System Operations in a Multihost Environment




9.1.1.   MHFS Environment
         MHFS is a feature that is available by itself or as part of the XTC feature grouping. The
         acronym MHFS and the term file sharing are used interchangeably in Unisys
         documentation; for example, the MHFS environment and the file-sharing environment
         both refer to an operations environment in which two or more hosts can access Exec
         files on shared mass storage devices. You must use the Multi-Host File Sharing
         (MHFS) Planning, Installation, and Operations Guide (7830 7469) as the starting point
         for dealing with operations in a multihost environment.

         In a file-sharing environment, there is a shared master file directory (MFD) that defines
         all files cataloged on shared mass storage devices; the directory-ID for these files is
         SHARED#. There is also a local (standard) MFD for each host that defines the host’s
         local set of cataloged files; the directory-ID for a local file is STD#. For more
         information on files and file directories, see Section 9.3.

         All shared devices in the file-sharing environment contain shared files only and are
         physically connected to shared mass storage control units, which allow all hosts to
         access the devices. A host can remain in the MHFS configuration as long as it has at
         least one path available to each shared subsystem. (It is not necessary for all hosts to
         be connected to all shared control units.) Host LAN Controller (HLC) connections link
         all hosts in a network MHFS configuration. An XPC links all hosts in an XPC MHFS
         configuration.

         File sharing is set up and maintained host by host, not application group by application
         group. A file-sharing host is identified by an alphabetic host-ID—A, B, C... Each host
         has a set of internal tables indicating the identity and status of the other hosts in the
         environment. A host is not officially in the file-sharing environment until the other
         hosts have been informed of its presence; a host can be up to the Exec without being
         up and active in the file-sharing environment. When a host leaves the environment,
         other hosts also need to have their internal tables updated to indicate that the host is
         no longer present; a host can be down to the Exec without being down to file sharing
         (from the perspective of other MHFS hosts). If the tables are not correct, you cannot
         get accurate information in response to keyins about the status of other hosts or in
         other console messages. For more information on file-sharing hosts, see Section 9.2.

         MHFS provides batch and demand access to shared Exec files, but XTC (Section 9.1.2)
         is needed to provide transaction access to shared databases belonging to TIP or UDS
         application groups.




9–2                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                         System Operations in a Multihost Environment




9.1.2. XTC Environment
         XTC is an XTPA feature grouping that provides improved capacity for TIP and UDS
         systems. For an introduction to XTC, see the Integrated Recovery Reference and
         Administration Guide for Multihost Environments (7831 0919).

         The MHFS feature is included in the XTC feature grouping. Because of the XTCEXEC
         feature, multiple hosts can access shared TIP or UDS databases concurrently. An
         XPC-L controls access to the database files from all hosts, to preserve database
         integrity. All hosts are informed of changes to the databases and to the transaction
         environment. The end result is a system that can process many more transactions
         than a one-host system could.

         The XPC-L also handles interhost broadcast messages as well as lock requests.

         The UDS product release includes the Receiver of UDS Broadcast (RUB) program,
         which must be active on all XTC hosts to permit proper communication between
         application groups on the host and UDS. See the Integrated Recovery Reference and
         Administration Guide for Multihost Environments (7831 0919) for information on
         starting the RUB run.

         The ARC feature is an optional part of the XTC feature grouping. Recovery runstreams
         registered through the ARC feature automatically recover and restart application
         groups and software components such as UDS and MCB. Heartbeat runstreams (one
         for each application group) detect any failure and start the appropriate recovery
         runstreams. If one host fails, runstreams can perform short recovery for local
         application groups and for concurrent application groups (see Section 9.4) on that host
         after the host recovers. In an XTC environment, ARC does not provide shared
         (host-to-host) short recovery. If a component fails but the host does not, the system
         automatically detects the failure and recovers the component.

         The ARC feature consists of the application interface bank (AIB) and the monitor run, a
         real-time program that communicates with the AIB. See Section 9.8 for information on
         starting and controlling the monitor run. The heartbeat run and the monitor services
         control program (MSCP) are also real-time programs. For detailed operations
         information on all of these programs, see the Integrated Recovery Reference and
         Administration Guide for Multihost Environments (7831 0919).

         Concurrent TIP and UDS application groups are defined on all XTC hosts and can be
         active on all hosts at the same time. See Section 9.4 for more information on
         application groups and Section 9.6 for information on TIP File Control.

         On an XTC system, time must be coordinated across hosts. Refer to the Exec System
         Software Administration Reference Manual (7831 0323) for more information on time
         management. You can use the D keyin (see Section 11) to display the date and time on
         a host, but you cannot use D keyins to alter the time.




7831 0281–034                                                                                9–3
System Operations in a Multihost Environment




9.1.3. Using the SPAIR Utility in an XPC or ARC Environment
        You may use the Software Product Application Initialization and Recovery (SPAIR)
        utility if you have the Automatic Recovery of Components (ARC) software. ARC is
        included with the Extended Transaction Capacity (XTC) feature grouping.

        If the audit trail is configured as removable mass storage, , then when the audit trail is
        opened or recovered, you can supply up to three pack-IDs for each leg (legs 1 and 2),
        or choose to execute the SPAIR utility to provide the initial equipment type and more
        or less than three pack-ids for each leg.

        The Exec System Software Administration Reference Manual (7831 0323) explains
        how to use the SPAIR utility. The manual describes SPAIR security, the SPAIR
        processor call, how to execute SPAIR commands, how to use SPAIR in a runstream,
        and how to interpret SPAIR messages.


9.2. Hosts and Shared Devices
        The Exec maintains the shared environment automatically unless you down Multi-Host
        File Sharing. After a host failure, or after one host is manually removed from file
        sharing, the remaining hosts stay in the MHFS environment and have access to TIP
        and UDS files on shared mass storage.

        The MHFS status of a host is one of the following:

            The host is up and active in the file-sharing environment (UP).
            The host is down to MHFS (DN).
            MHFS is being brought down gracefully on this host (RV).

        To determine the status of a single host in file-sharing environment, use this keyin:

            MH,FS HOST,host-id

        where host-id is A, B, C...

        To determine the host-ID and status of all hosts in file-sharing environment, use this
        keyin:

            MH,FS HOST,ALL

        To see the host-ID and site-ID of this host and all hosts in the file-sharing environment,
        use this keyin:

            MH,FS HOST

        The RV status can be displayed only as the status of the host on which you enter the
        MH,FS HOST keyin. The status of all other hosts is displayed as either UP or DN to
        MHFS.




9–4                                                                                7831 0281–034
                                          System Operations in a Multihost Environment




         To determine whether file sharing is up at all, you can use either an MH,FS HOST or an
         MH,FS HOST,ALL keyin.

         To determine the status of all devices active in the file-sharing environment, use this
         keyin:

             FS,SHRD

         To determine the status of all shared mass storage devices, use this keyin:

             MS,SHRD

         For detailed information on shared files, shared devices, and MHFS features, see the
         Multi-Host File Sharing (MHFS) Planning, Installation, and Operations Guide (7830
         7469). Although MH keyins are documented in Section 11 of this manual, do not use
         these keyins for file-sharing control until you become familiar with the information in
         the MHFS Guide, which is summarized as follows:

             The MH,IN DIR,SHARED keyin causes shared file initialization and initialization of
              the shared MCT when you boot a local host and no other hosts are up in the
              file-sharing environment.
             The MH,RC DIR,SHARED keyin causes shared file reconfiguration and initialization
              of the shared MCT when no other hosts are active in the file-sharing environment.
             The MH,AD directory-id, device-id keyin changes the directory-ID of a device
              from shared to local or from local to shared. Note the specified device must be
              marked down.
             The MH,UP keyin adds a host dynamically to the file-sharing environment while the
              host is running and then notifies the other active hosts of the addition. If the host
              was previously in the MHFS environment, then this keyin causes a shared
              recovery if no other hosts are up in the MHFS environment or a shared
              reconfiguration if other hosts are up.
             The MH,DN keyin is coordinated across all hosts to physically and logically remove
              a host from file sharing. The removal of the host immediately terminates all runs
              with shared files assigned and prevents future shared file assignments. (This keyin
              results in an XER055 stop in an XTC environment.)
             The MH,RV keyin removes a host from the file-sharing environment by preventing
              new shared file assignments. The host is completely removed when existing
              shared file assignments are freed. (This keyin results in an XER055 stop in an XTC
              environment.)
             The MH,DN HOST,host-id keyin is executed from one host to mark another host
              down to file-sharing. Do not attempt to use the MH,DN HOST,host-ID keyin to
              remove another host from the file-sharing environment.

         You can use the HK,host-id keyin to execute unsolicited keyins on other MHFS hosts
         from one MHFS host. The responses to the unsolicited keyins are displayed on the
         console of the host on which the HK keyin is executed. You can also use the
         HM,host-id keyin to send console messages to other hosts.

         MV keyins are not allowed in a file-sharing environment.


7831 0281–034                                                                                   9–5
System Operations in a Multihost Environment




        MHFS console messages are described in the System Console Messages Reference
        Manual (7833 3275).

        As mentioned in Section 9.1.2, in an XTC environment you can use the D keyin (see
        Section 11) to display the date and time on a host, but you cannot use D keyins to alter
        the time. For information on establishing time coordination among XTC hosts, see the
        Exec System Software Administration Reference Manual (7831 0323).


9.3. Files and File Directories
        The directory-ID for a cataloged file depends on the type of storage on which it
        resides. If the file resides on shared mass storage, it has a directory-ID of SHARED#
        and is defined in the shared master file directory (MFD). If the file resides on a host’s
        local mass storage, it has a directory-ID of STD# (standard, meaning local) and is
        defined in the local MFD for that host. For an Exec file registered with TIP, there is
        another set of TIP Directories: shared TIP directory and local TIP directory. There is no
        direct correspondence between the MFD and TIP directories. The local TIP directory
        might contain files cataloged on local and shared mass storage. However, the shared
        TIP directory can only contain files cataloged on shared mass storage. See Section 9.6
        for more information on TIP directories.

        Database files belonging to a concurrent (for XTC) TIP or UDS application group are on
        shared mass storage, and if TIP, in the shared TIP directory. System files that belong to
        concurrent and local application groups vary in regards to local or shared mass
        storage. To find out which system or Integrated Recovery files exist for each one of
        these types of application groups, and where they are located, see the Integrated
        Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for Multihost Environments (7831
        0919).

        To determine whether a cataloged file is currently assigned or if it even exists, you
        can use this keyin:

          FF [directory-id#]qualifier*file.

        Because of the MHFS double-MFD search feature, if you do not specify the
        directory-ID, the system tries to locate the file by checking the local MFD first and
        then the shared MFD, unless a feature has been installed that changes this behavior. In
        the response to the keyin, the system tells you whether a common name section
        (CNS), such as EXEC8 (the Exec run-ID), a run, or a host has the file assigned. To
        protect shared files in an XTC environment, the MHFS application-private assignment
        feature allows the Exec to assign shared TIP/Exec files exclusively and UDS to assign
        shared UDS/Exec files exclusively, but this assignment is allowed on multiple hosts.
        For information on the FF keyin, see Section 11; for information on application-private
        assignment, see the Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for
        Multihost Environments (7831 0919).




9–6                                                                               7831 0281–034
                                           System Operations in a Multihost Environment




9.4. Application Groups
         There are two types of application groups (AG)—local and concurrent

             A local application group is known to only one host and shares no resources
              (including its database) with other hosts.
             In an XTC environment (Section 9.1.2), a concurrent application group is defined on
              both hosts and can be active on both hosts at the same time. All XTC hosts can
              access the database for the application group concurrently.
              An AG/host is the portion of a concurrent application group that exists on a
              particular XTC host. For example, concurrent TIP application group 4 exists on host
              A as AG4/A and on host B as AG4/B. The application group must be recovered
              separately on each host—that is, each AG/host must be recovered separately.
              Note that the term AG/host refers only to the portion of a concurrent application
              group that exists on a particular XTC host and does not refer to a local application
              group configured on a particular host.

         The information presented here covers only concurrent application groups because
         they exist only in special multihost environments.

         The status of concurrent AG/host can be one of the following:

             Up (UP)
              The AG/host has been initialized or recovered, and it is ready for processing. All
              database and associated system files are available and assigned.
             Down (DN)
              The AG/host is not available; queue-item storage has been released and the audit
              trail has been closed (if it was open). SUTIL or INIT recovery can be used to
              recover the AG/host. The ARF file remains assigned.
             Recovery in progress (RCV)
              The AG/host is being recovered; it is available for processing when recovery is
              complete.
             Deferred (DF)
              The AG/host is not available and all its files, including the ARF files have been freed
              from Step Control. If you have also deferred the audit trail, the ARF is then freed
              by Audit Control and by the Exec.

         To determine the status of and other information about a concurrent AG/host, use this
         keyin:

             AP n FS

         The format of the console message displayed in response to the AP nn FS keyin is

             APP n state qitem-usage PRG:steps REC:type MCB:MCB-status
             APP n TYPE: ag-type DR: DR-flag READY STEPS: num-ready




7831 0281–034                                                                                      9–7
System Operations in a Multihost Environment




        This message describes the status of application group nn on this particular host. The
        fields in the message are explained in Section 11, in the description of the AP keyin.
        The second line of the message appears only in a multihost environment: TYPE is the
        application group type (LOCAL or CONCURRENT) and DR is the database recovery (DR)
        flag, which contains the status of database recovery.

        These are some of the actions that you can perform to change the state of an
        application group:

           Bring up (enable) a concurrent AG/host with an AP n UP keyin.
           Bring down (disable) a concurrent AG/host with an AP n DN keyin.
           Defer a concurrent A/Ghost with an AP n DF keyin.

        See the Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for Multihost
        Environments (7831 0919) for specific application group recovery information. For
        general application group recovery information, see Section 7, "Step Control Recovery
        and Initialization Options," in this manual and the Integrated Recovery Utility
        Operations Guide (7830 8194). The AP keyins are documented in Section 11 of this
        manual.


9.5. Audit Trails
        A local application may be configured with an audit trail. If an audit trail is configured,
        then for each audit trail, there is one ARF and one Audit Control Interface (ACI) file.

        In an XTC environment (Section 9.1.2), each concurrent AG/host has an audit trail, so
        each concurrent application group has multiple audit trails, one for each AG/host; the
        IRU blends all of the AG/host audit trails together when recovering the database for
        the concurrent application group. For each audit trail, there is one ARF and one ACI file.

        The audit trail for an application group must be open or available to be opened (that is,
        closed) for recoverable transaction programs to run. AG/host or application group
        recovery also usually requires that the audit trail be available for auditing.

        For a discussion of Audit Control, audit trail states, and AT keyins, see Section 7. For a
        description of all AT keyins, see Section 11.


9.6. TIP File Control, Scheduling, and Automatic
     Recovery
        An XTC system has both local TIP File Control and shared TIP File Control. Shared and
        local TIP File Controls are handled separately. Local TIP File Control can be initialized or
        recovered. Shared TIP File Control can be initialized or reemployed. For information on
        messages that you might see while initializing or reemploying shared TIP File Control,
        see "Multihost Initialization, Recovery, and Reemployment" in the Transaction
        Processing Administration and Operations Reference Manual (7830 7881). For
        specific operations procedures and scenarios in an XTC environment, see the
        Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for Multihost
        Environments (7831 0919).



9–8                                                                                  7831 0281–034
                                          System Operations in a Multihost Environment




         Only TIP/Exec files cataloged on shared mass storage can be registered in the shared
         TIP directory. TIP files reserved in TIP/Exec files that are registered in the shared TIP
         directory are automatically reserved in the shared TIP directory. TIP/Exec files
         cataloged on local mass storage are registered in a local TIP directory, but TIP/Exec
         files cataloged on shared mass storage can also be registered in a local TIP directory;
         the TIP files reserved in those TIP/Exec files are automatically reserved in the local TIP
         directory. For a TIP/Exec file, the directory-ID refers to the TIP directory in which it is
         registered, not to the MFD indicating the type of mass storage on which it resides; a
         local TIP/Exec file can reside on shared mass storage. The same is true of the TIP files
         reserved in the TIP/Exec file.

         On a one-host TIP system, there is one validation table (VALTAB) with entries defining
         all transaction programs, in TIP system file 4. On an XTC system, there is only VALTAB,
         so TIP system file 4 is shared. There is also a single set of TIP program files (SUPUR
         files for TIP programs, TPUR files for HVTIP program libraries, or standard online
         program files). TIP scheduling is a system-wide resource on an XTC system; however,
         you enable or disable it host by host. Some flagbox settings are broadcast to all hosts,
         while others affect only the local host. For details on the effects of flagbox bits in an
         XTC environment, see the flagbox section in the Transaction Processing
         Administration and Operations Reference Manual (7830 7881) (the FB keyin is
         documented in that section and also in Section 11 of this manual).

         The TIP automatic recovery feature is required for XTC. This feature automatically
         records the state of the TIP environment as it changes, so that TIP recovery from a
         system failure can be accomplished without manual procedures. TIP automatic
         recovery recovers the TIP scheduling and program loading environment (such as the
         flagbox and HVTIP libraries that were loaded).

         On a one-host TIP system, there is one TIP automatic recovery file (ARF). On an XTC
         system, there is also one TIP ARF, which is located on shared mass storage. Each time
         a host enters the XTC environment, you must enable TIP automatic recovery manually
         with a TIP UP keyin. From that point on, the feature functions automatically.

         For detailed information on TIP automatic recovery and on the use of TIP UP, TIP DN,
         and TIP RS keyins, see "TIP Automatic Recovery and TIP Keyins" in the Transaction
         Processing Administration and Operations Reference Manual (7830 7881). The TIP
         keyins are also documented in Section 11 of this manual.


                                                 Caution
                The configuration parameter TPLIB contains the TIP file number that
                contains the first HVTIP library. This parameter can be changed dynamically.
                In an XTC environment, failing to keep TPLIB set to the same value on each
                host in the XTC environment can result in TIP scheduling errors, TIP
                program aborts, or TIP Automatic Recovery failure.




7831 0281–034                                                                                   9–9
System Operations in a Multihost Environment




9.7. ARC and the Monitor Run
        ARC, an optional feature of XTC, uses a monitor run to monitor the heartbeat of
        application group components and to start the appropriate recovery runstream if a
        component fails. The monitor run includes a primary run and a backup run. If the
        primary run fails, the backup run becomes the primary run and another backup run is
        created.

        To start the monitor run, you use an @START statement or an ST keyin. Once the
        monitor run is active, you can use ARCMR keyins to determine the status of the
        monitor run and of application group components, and to terminate the run. See the
        Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for Multihost
        Environments (7831 0919) for detailed operations information on the monitor run. The
        ARCMR keyin is also documented in Section 11 of this manual.


9.8. Extended Transaction Processing
        In an XTC system, an extended transaction processor (XPC) controls access to the
        database files from all hosts. For XTC, the XPC is configured as a control unit and is in
        the UP or the UP NA state. Each host has two or more operational paths to the XPC.
        To bring the XPC up or down, you use UP or DN keyins.

        If the XPC is unavailable, the system must run in non-XPC mode with one host
        designated as the non-XPC host. For a description of non-XPC mode and procedures
        to follow on all hosts, see the Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration
        Guide for Multihost Environments (7831 0919).

        See the Integrated Recovery Reference and Administration Guide for Multihost
        Environments (7831 0919) for information on XPC preparation. The guide also
        describes XTC operations scenarios involving the XPC. See Section 11 of this manual
        for descriptions of UP and DN keyins and the System Console Messages Reference
        Manual (7833 3275) for descriptions of XPC messages.


9.9. Shared Unit Duplexing
        Unit Duplexing can be used as a stand-alone feature with MHFS, with MHFS and XTC
        Applications feature grouping. Duplex associations between local devices and shared
        devices can only be established dynamically.

        Keyins used with Unit Duplexing are described in Section 11, and messages are
        described in the System Console Messages Reference Manual (7833 3275). For
        detailed information on Unit Duplexing, see the Unit Duplexing Planning, Installation,
        and Operations Overview (7830 7592).

        If you use an FS device keyin to determine the status of a shared device that is
        unit-duplexed, the response to the keyin has this format:

          device1 -UD- device2 S status [*] F PACKID packed [EXEC|SHARED DRS]




9–10                                                                               7831 0281–034
                                        System Operations in a Multihost Environment




         Since the devices are unit-duplexed, they both have the same status, which can be UP
         or SU; a unit-duplexed device cannot be RV. S indicates that the devices are shared,
         and F indicates that they are prepped as fixed.

         To establish or reestablish a duplex association between devices that are configured
         as shared, use the UD keyin:

           UD device1,device2

         where device2 is the device that you want to be an exact copy of device1.




7831 0281–034                                                                             9–11
System Operations in a Multihost Environment
